Christa Pike’s Execution Doctor Hadn't Performed IV Procedure in 13 Years

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The physician who oversaw Christa Pike’s execution was also a part of a failed execution just a few months prior, and it was revealed that he hadn’t performed the key IV procedure for 13 years before the two recent attempts.

On May 21, Dr. Mark Fowler and three members of an intravenous team spent over an hour struggling to insert two IV lines during the planned execution of death row inmate Tony Carruthers.

The execution was eventually called off, and Fowler was asked to serve the same role just four months later for Pike’s execution on September 30.

Carruthers’ lawyer Maria DeLiberto said in a court-filed declaration that just 30 minutes before his execution was scheduled to take place, the IV team had still not established the two peripheral IV sites that were needed to lethally inject the inmate, according to People.

DeLiberto claimed that Fowler had been called into the room to assist in the execution, despite the fact that the TDOC Lethal Injection Execution Protocol states that the physician is only tasked with “determining if the inmate is deceased” and “establishing central line IV if necessary.”

Fowler reportedly attempted to insert a central line in two locations, but was unsuccessful both times.

By the time Carruthers’ execution was called off, he was “clearly in pain” and “gutturally groaning” as he said that it “hurts,” according to the attorney.

Just one year before Carruthers’ failed execution, Fowler said in a 2025 deposition that he did not have hospital privileges or privileges to insert a central IV line at any medical facility, according to People.

He added that the last time he performed the procedure with those privileges was in 2013, and he had not set a central line since then.

Gail A. Van Norman, M.D. — professor emeritus of anesthesiology and pain medicine at the University of Washington — said that Fowler “does not have the current experience or training that would be required to obtain or retain privileges to place central lines at many, if not most, major healthcare institutions today.”

Norman added in her deposition that the method Fowler used when inserting a central line is a “violation of the current standard of care.”

Following Pike’s failed execution, Fowler said she survived because an IV was improperly placed and failed to send the pentobarbital into her veins and instead went into her tissues.

“The investigation into why Tennessee keeps botching executions should have been launched in May when TDOC subjected Tony Carruthers to more than an hour of pain and suffering before his execution had to be called off,” Kit Thomas, Deputy Chief of the Capital Habeas Unit for the Federal Public Defender, Middle District of Tennessee, said.

Thomas added that Pike’s “botched execution happened after state officials ignored our warnings about their flawed protocol.”

“These problems will keep happening until Tennessee takes meaningful steps to increase transparency, oversight, and accountability in the execution process. Executions must not resume until it does,” Thomas concluded.

While Pike, 50, is currently in stable condition, her future is not yet known, as Tennessee Gov. Bill Lee paused all executions in the state after an investigation was launched into what went wrong.

The Vitamin Deficiency Linked To Chronic Headaches

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Almost four in five people with chronic migraine have low levels of this vitamin.

Almost four in five people with chronic migraine have low levels of this vitamin.

Frequent headaches are linked to lower vitamin D levels.

People with chronic migraine have lower levels than those with occasional migraines or no headache disorder.

Lower vitamin D is also associated with greater muscle tenderness.

The vitamin helps muscles and nerves function, making it relevant to understanding pain.

One possibility is that low levels amplify sensitivity to pain, although this remains an explanation to investigate.

The research compared around 150 adults, divided equally between chronic migraine, episodic migraine and a group without headache disorders.

Blood tests showed low vitamin D in 78 percent of the chronic migraine group, compared with 60 percent of those with episodic migraine and 46 percent of the comparison group.

Average levels were lowest in chronic migraine and highest among those without headaches.

Chronic migraine means headaches on at least 15 days a month for more than three months, with migraine features on at least eight days.

These headaches can change in character, even within the same day, and can include pain resembling tension headaches.

Lower vitamin D levels were associated with more tenderness when muscles were pressed.

The researchers suggest that muscular discomfort and changes in pain processing could interact, potentially helping sustain headaches.

However, the association could also run in the opposite direction: people with migraine may spend less time outdoors because of light sensitivity or fatigue.

The findings do not establish that supplements prevent migraines.

Vitamin D’s established functions extend beyond maintaining strong bones.

It helps the body absorb calcium, supports muscle movement and allows nerves to carry messages between the brain and the body.

Deficiency can cause a condition called osteomalacia in adults, involving bone pain and muscle weakness.

The body produces vitamin D when sunlight reaches the skin, while food provides another source.

Oily fish, including salmon and mackerel, are among the best natural sources, while egg yolks contain smaller amounts.

Some breakfast cereals and other foods are fortified with the vitamin.

People who rarely expose their skin to sunlight can have more difficulty obtaining enough.

A blood test measures vitamin D status, helping distinguish a nutritional shortage from symptoms with other possible explanations.

The study was published in the journal Cephalalgia Reports (Prakash et al., 2026).

Psychologist, Jeremy Dean, PhD is the founder and author of PsyBlog. He holds a doctorate in psychology from University College London and two other advanced degrees in psychology. He has been writing about scientific research on PsyBlog since 2004. View all posts by Dr Jeremy Dean

Alan Jones, 1980 Formula 1 World Champion, Has Died

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The Australian world champion was 80 years old.

Alan Jones, an Australian racing icon and the 1980 Formula 1 world champion, has died after a brief illness. He had just turned 80 years old.

Jones took a long route from Australia to Formula 1, moving to Europe first in 1967 and working through a variety of odd series before debuting in a privately-owned Hesketh in 1975. He then ran in stints for Graham Hill and John Surtees before joining Shadow, one of the strangest teams in F1 history. He would win the 1977 Austrian Grand Prix for the team, his first victory and what would be the only win the team ever claimed in F1. Jones finished the season in seventh, and suddenly became a major player in the series.

In 1978, he joined a recently re-formed Williams Grand Prix Engineering. This was the second F1 team run by Frank Williams, one that was not yet a powerhouse. Williams became a force to be reckoned with quickly after Jones joined, allowing the Australian driver to establish himself as a player in F1 with four wins in the 1979 season. That number ballooned to five in 1980, earning him the title by 13 points over future Williams champion Nelson Piquet. He also guided the team to its first constructor’s title.

Jones missed out on the 1981 driver’s title, but he and teammate Carlos Reutemann guided the Williams operation to its second of what has since become nine constructor’s championships. He retired after that year, although he would occasionally show up on the grid in parts of three more seasons.

In the decades that followed, Jones became a mainstay in the Australian touring car championship that evolved into what we now know as Supercars. He won five races in a career that stretched until 2002, entering the Bathurst 1000 19 times. Jones never won that race, nor did he win the 24 Hours of Le Mans in his two starts in that event, but he remained a presence in auto racing for decades.

Australia’s Nine reports that Jones received a cancer diagnosis “only weeks ago.” He is survived by his wife and four children.

Fred Smith's love of cars comes from his fascination with auto racing. Unfortunately, that passion led him to daily drive a high-mileage, first-year Porsche Panamera. He is still thinking about the last lap of the 2011 Indianapolis 500.

Birmingham City told to strike Nottingham Forest transfer for 95-cap international star

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Birmingham City's impressive start to the season has got many fans at St. Andrew's @ Knighthead Park dreaming of a return to the Premier League for the first time since 2011.

Chris Davies' side have only lost two of their first 14 matches of the Championship season, winning four of their last five and scoring 14 goals in the process.

Tuesday's controlled 3-0 win over Millwall saw Birmingham reach the automatic promotion places for just over 24 hours, before they were knocked down to third for the time being after Wolves' win at Bristol City.

Such a strong start wasn't exactly forecast by many in this part of the Second City at the beginning of the season, as it was argued that the squad was left short in certain areas.

However, given the well-documented ambitions of American investors, Knighthead Capital Management, many are now debating what activity will be conducted in the winter window if Blues are still among the promotion contenders.

With Birmingham's forward department currently made up of August Priske, Jay Stansfield, Luis Vazquez and Marvin Ducksch, it has been questioned what changes will be made going forward.

Priske has already netted six times this season whilst Stansfield has now firmly aclimatised to playing as a 'number 10', whilst exit talk continues to surround Ducksch after a failed summer departure to Valencia, and Vazquez is yet to convince the majority of supporters.

As such, it was recently debated by Blues YouTuber, Tommy Kelsall, whether a reunion for Chris Wood, who scored 11 times in 29 appearances on loan in the 2011/12 season under Chris Hughton, makes sense, given his similarities with Priske, but also the experience under his belt.

The in-form striker was absent from Tuesday's dominant success over Millwall

Wood has only made two Premier League starts under Oliver Glasner at Nottingham Forest this season, having seen his 2025/26 campaign blighted by a knee injury.

Beforehand, the 95-time New Zealand international scored 20 times as the Reds secured European football in 2024/25, and his record of 170 strikes in the top two divisions is indisputable.

With his contract at the City Ground up next June, Football League World's Birmingham City fan pundit, Jason Moore, believes the 34-year-old would be a great option for Davies to call upon.

"I don't mind the shout," Moore told FLW.

"People might say he's aging, but he's, again, a proven Premier League striker. He's done a decent job in the Premier League.

"He's not pacey. But, I don't think that's his game and he'd probably be, like that tweet says, a like-for-like for August Priske in the way we play, so I'd be all up for it.

"You know he's going to work hard, he'd take that pressure off Priske playing every single game, and he's a miles better replacement than Luis Vazquez.

"If you can look at Chris Wood and if he's not playing at Forest, then I'd be all for it. If we could pull that off, that's a great shout. Him or Che (Adams), I'd be all up for it."

Blues fans, should Chris Davies attempt to sign Chris Wood in January? Comment below!

"I'd narrowly favour Che for his pace, but I'd be more than happy with Chris Wood if Che wasn't available. Both are excellent options."

Having a focal point in attack such as Priske has allowed Birmingham to source goals from across Davies' squad, and whilst the Dane has become a feared striker this term, Wood's experience would certainly aid a promotion bid.

Like Priske, he has the ability to bring fellow attackers into the game and make their own deep runs into the box and create chances, whilst also having a poacher's instinct despite not being particularly fast.

If Birmingham were also to end their Premier League exile, then a player of such calibre would also be worth retaining, given Wood's extensive record in both divisions.

The nature of the Championship schedule also means that the forward is likely to source more minutes in the West Midlands than he currently is in the East.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

The Top 30 Ski Resorts in the West (2027)

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Choosing a ski resort for your next trip is about more than snow totals and vertical drop. Sure, you want great snow, lifts that keep you moving, and terrain that makes you want to take one more lap. But last winter reminded us just how much conditions can shape a ski experience—and how difficult it can be to judge a resort when much of its terrain isn’t open or skiing the way it should. A lean snow year across much of the West made for a challenging season, and those conditions appear to have left their mark on this year’s rankings, even at some of North America’s most celebrated destinations.

Every spring, we ask readers to weigh in on the resorts they’ve visited, rating their experiences across 18 categories that span the full spectrum of a ski vacation. From snow, grooming, lifts, and terrain variety to dining, lodging, nightlife, and overall character, these rankings reflect the collective wisdom of skiers who’ve experienced these places firsthand. But this year’s results come with an added wrinkle: When terrain is limited and conditions are far from ideal, it’s not always easy to separate a resort’s inherent strengths from the circumstances of the season. The latest survey went out last April, and the results are in.

More Results: Looking For the East Rankings? Click Here.

The 2027 West rankings, below, reveal which resorts are getting the most right, where they truly shine, and where there’s room to improve. Some familiar names took significant tumbles. Others weathered the challenges and climbed higher. These shifts suggest that even resorts with exceptional terrain, amenities, and reputations aren’t immune to a difficult winter. At the same time, these are overall vacation-experience rankings, not a referendum on snow alone. Our reviews break down each resort’s biggest strengths and weaknesses—on and off the slopes—to help you figure out which destination best fits what you’re looking for in a ski trip.

Think of this as advice from your fellow skiers, informed by a winter that didn’t always make for easy comparisons. These rankings aren’t a one-size-fits-all verdict on where you should spend your winter vacation, nor do they tell the whole story of what a resort can deliver in a better snow year. Explore the rankings, see how your favorites stack up, and discover a few places that might deserve a spot on your list. And if you disagree with the results, even better. The Reader Resort Survey returns next spring, giving you another chance to weigh in and help shape next year’s Resort Guide.

New this season, SKI is partnering with Outside Travel to provide seamless hotel-booking options at many of the Top 50 resorts. Navigate to the resort’s personalized landing page next to the “Book Now” bullet to explore lodging by budget and/or location. More destinations are coming online soon, plus revisit for dining and après recommendations from the SKI editors. Join Outside+ for up to 10% off lodging rates.

Big-mountain skiing meets a bona fide ski town, with enough terrain, dining, nightlife, and family-friendly diversions to keep everyone happy—if you can navigate the crowds.

Don’t let Park City’s No. 30 ranking fool you: This resort has a lot going for it. Readers praise its sheer size, varied terrain, and off-mountain amenities, making it one of the West’s most well-rounded destinations for groups with different abilities and interests. With more than 7,300 acres of lift-served terrain, there’s room for just about everyone, from families cruising groomers and exploring beginner-friendly zones to experts hunting steep, gladed pitches like Condor Woods and the high-alpine bowls of McConkey’s.

Then there’s the town itself. Historic Main Street is the real deal, packed with restaurants, bars, shops, and places to stay, while the broader resort scene offers plenty to do long after the lifts close. Park City scores among the best in nightlife, après, dining, and lodging, and readers appreciate that non-skiers can have a vacation here, too. Families also give the destination high marks, thanks to its mix of terrain and off-slope activities. “I love how close the resort is to town and all the restaurants and other amenities,” commented one survey respondent.

Access is another major advantage. Salt Lake City International Airport is close enough that visitors can often land and be skiing within hours, making Park City an easy choice for a long weekend or a family trip. “It’s the easiest large resort to get to in the U.S. with an incredible amount of terrain,” said one SKI reader. But that convenience is a double-edged sword: Crowds can clog base areas and lift lines, while parking headaches and high prices leave readers wishing the overall experience were a better value. Lift bottlenecks remain a particular sore spot, but lift network upgrades coming for the 2027-’28 season hope to move the needle.

This season brings significant upgrades at Canyons Village, including a new enclosed gondola to replace the aging Cabriolet and a five-story parking garage. Those changes should improve the arrival experience, though new parking fees will add another cost for some visitors. Through it all, Park City’s appeal is undeniable: enormous terrain, a lively town, and easy access from a major airport. “It’s a big mountain with lots of options,” said one Park City skier. “Fun and varied terrain and (usually) great snow.”

“Good for both hard-chargers and solid intermediates.  Lots of varied terrain.  Easy to navigate without taking a lot of lifts.”

At Canyons Village, a new enclosed 10-passenger Skyway Gondola replaces the open-air Cabriolet, with a mid-station serving nearby developments and a five-story garage is expected to open in early December with $29 per day (free after noon and for carpools of four-plus).

On the events front, Shaun White’s The Snow League comes to the mountain Jan. 21–23, 2027, bringing elite halfpipe snowboarding and freeskiing competition, live music, and fan events. Park City has rebuilt its 22-foot Eagle Superpipe for the competition, and the pipe will open to the public after the event.

Jaw-dropping Lake Tahoe views, sprawling terrain, and a lively après scene make Heavenly as much about the good times off the slopes as the skiing itself.

At Heavenly, the scenery is almost as memorable as the skiing. Perched above Lake Tahoe, with terrain straddling the California-Nevada border, the resort delivers one spectacular lake view after another across 5,000 acres of skiable terrain. There’s plenty for a range of abilities, from mellow groomers for families and newer skiers to wide-open blues and approachable black diamonds. Even longtime locals can’t resist pausing to take in the view. “I’ve lived in the area for almost 20 years and those lake views still almost knock me off my skis,” confessed one Heavenly fan.

Heavenly earns high marks for nightlife, dining, and lodging, with stellar après spots, craft breweries, and live music around South Lake Tahoe, plus the casinos and entertainment of nearby Stateline, Nevada. Whether you’re looking to keep the party going or settle in for a long dinner after a day on the mountain, there’s plenty to do beyond skiing.

The biggest frustration? Getting around. Readers dinged Heavenly for crowded lifts, including the gondola, and guest service remains an area for improvement. Some base-area facilities could also use a refresh. Still, those complaints have a way of fading when you crest a ridge, turn a corner, and find Lake Tahoe stretched out below you in all its glory. “There’s no shortage of beautiful runs—especially when the sun is shining and the lake is visible from anywhere on top of the mountain.”

“Heavenly has great grooming systems, so no matter how bad the snow gets, the groomers are fun to ride.”

A family-friendly favorite with forgiving groomers, surprisingly adventurous corners, and one of the easiest getaways from Denver.

As the closest major Epic Pass resort to Denver, Keystone draws plenty of Front Range locals alongside visitors looking for a full mountain vacation. The good news is that both are on tap at this family favorite. The frontside can get busy, particularly on weekends, but there’s more to Keystone than its popular cruisers. Head farther out to explore The Outback or venture into Bergman Bowl, where high-alpine terrain offers adventurous intermediates and families a chance to explore beyond the usual groomer circuit. “Love Keystone and its low-key nature,” shared one fan. “Fun terrain, especially Bergman Bowl and the glades on The Outback.”

For families, Keystone has a lot to offer. Free parking, efficient lifts, a strong ski school, and a location just 90 minutes from Denver make the logistics easier, while base-area wagons help parents haul gear and tired kids. The village isn’t the most authentic, but it has what skiers need, plus fun-for-families extras such as character parades and seasonal events. Don’t miss the annual snow fort atop Dercum Mountain—it’s always a hit with younger skiers. “Keystone is the unquestionable gem of the I-70 corridor,” exclaimed one excited reader.

The resort also scores well for grooming, which is good news for skiers who like confidence-inspiring runs. But don’t assume the mountain is all mellow blues and beginner-friendly terrain. Stronger skiers can find challenging lines on North Peak plus glades in The Windows. Keystone may not rank among Colorado’s most demanding mountains, but those willing to venture beyond the frontside can find plenty to keep them interested. “There’s legit steep terrain here if you know where to look,” said a survey respondent.

Where Keystone falls short is local flavor. Its purpose-built village and convenient setup make for an easy vacation, but the atmosphere can feel more functional than distinctive compared with Colorado’s historic ski towns. Still, for families and Denver-area skiers who prize convenience, well-groomed runs, and straightforward logistics, Keystone remains a dependable choice.

“In a bad snow year (which this one was), Keystone’s snowmaking and grooming made it possible for them to outdistance everyone around them. This was the place to go all season. More than half of my total ski days were here this year.”

Keystone officially debuts Kindred Resort, its first premier luxury ski-in/ski-out hotel, at the base of the River Run Gondola. The new property brings upscale lodging and easy slope access to River Run Village.

Western spirit, family-friendly skiing, and a lively town make Steamboat a welcoming all-around getaway, even when the snow doesn’t cooperate.

Readers appreciate a resort that feels welcoming rather than intimidating, and Steamboat checks that box once again despite a small slip in the rankings. On the mountain, Steamboat offers a satisfying mix of groomers, tree skiing, and more adventurous terrain. Mahogany Ridge’s advanced and expert glades and bowls expand the options for stronger skiers, while the double-black glades Christmas Tree Bowl rewards those willing to venture beyond the cruisers. Still, challenge remains a weaker category in the survey, so experts seeking relentless steeps may want to temper their expectations.

For families and mixed-ability groups, though, the range of terrain—and the resort’s strong service culture—makes it easy to build a ski day that works for everyone. Steamboat scores high once again for its welcoming family vibe. The resort drives that point home with an enticing family-friendly perk offering free skiing for kids 12 and under from Nov. 21 through Dec. 15—no adult purchase required. “The resort really caters to families with young kids by offering some interesting areas for those kids to ski,” said one reader. “The ski school for young kids really leverages these areas, too.”

Steamboat’s other great asset is the town itself, but the much improved base village is beginning to give it a run for its money. The base has matured into a lively place to spend time after skiing, with upscale restaurants, an ice rink, casual slopeside bars, and a bit of nightlife that gives the resort a genuine après scene. But, downtown Steamboat Springs is still a huge draw. “Love the cool cowboy vibe downtown!” exclaimed one fan. It’s a place that retains its own identity, from Western art galleries to legendary F.M. Light & Sons, where you can shop for cowboy hats, boots, knickknacks and more. It’s this blend of mountain convenience and authentic character that keeps Steamboat feeling distinct from the more generic resort villages.

Parking remains a frustration, particularly during busy periods, though new policies aim to encourage carpools and make demand easier to manage. The resort’s popularity can also mean crowds, but recent lift and base-area improvements have helped skiers get moving more efficiently. It’s these types of skier-first initiatives that help Steamboat remain a compelling choice for visitors looking for a welcoming Colorado vacation with plenty of Western flavor.

“It’s not a ‘see and be seen’ kind of ski resort. You go here because you love to ski or you want your family to love to ski. You love the idea of the Wild, Wild West and you’d love to be riding a horse across a high mountain plain. You appreciate the simple things in life, and while this place is getting more popular, you’re hoping it always keeps its mountain-town feel.”

Steamboat is adding Barrow’s Bend, a new beginner-friendly green trail connecting Rendezvous Lodge to Sunshine Peak Lift, and honoring late local Olympian Moose Barrows. They’re raising the Maverick’s Halfpipe base, reducing the snow needed to build it by an estimated 40 to 60 percent and helping it open earlier. Advanced skiers and riders can also sign up for a new full-day Heli-Ready guided experience designed to build skills and confidence for heli-ski terrain.

Getting here is getting easier, too, thanks to air service expanding to 18 major U.S. airports, including new flights from Portland, Ore., and additional options from Austin and Houston.

Six peaks of varied skiing meet a lively historic town packed with great restaurants, après, nightlife, and classic Colorado character.

Breckenridge dropped 10 spots in this year’s rankings, a slide likely influenced by last winter’s disappointing snow across much of the West. But one lean season can’t erase what makes Breck such a reliable all-around ski vacation. Across six peaks, the resort serves up everything from cruisey groomers to steep, high-alpine terrain, with enough variety to keep a mixed-ability group happy for days. “Breckenridge has something for every type of skier,” said one reader. “Mellow greens for beginners and kids, plenty of intermediate terrain, and very challenging hike-to terrain.”

From a vacation perspective, one of Breck’s main draws is all it has going on off the slopes. The historic town has the kind of energy that makes a trip feel like a proper getaway, with a walkable Main Street lined with independent shops, excellent restaurants, lively bars, and live music venues. Readers gave the resort high marks for lodging, dining, nightlife, and après, reflecting a destination where there’s plenty to do after the last chair. “I love the apres-ski at the base of Peak 8 and Peak 7,” shared one survey respondent. “And there are so many restaurants on Main Street depending on your taste and budget.”

The drawbacks are familiar to anyone who has visited during peak periods. Weekend crowds can snarl the base areas, parking is expensive and sometimes inconvenient, and the overall cost of a Breck vacation can add up quickly. On-mountain dining also tends to feel pricey for what you get. Savvy skiers know to start early, move away from the busiest base areas, and explore beyond the most obvious runs. The resort’s size helps, but timing still matters.

For skiers who want a mountain with plenty of terrain and a town that stays lively long after the lifts close, Breckenridge remains a solid choice. Its ranking may have taken a hit after a challenging snow year, but the combination of varied skiing, comfortable lodging, standout dining, and a genuine ski-town atmosphere keeps it firmly in the conversation for a full-fledged Colorado getaway. “You really can’t beat the variety of terrain and the town of Breckenridge is so quaint!”

“While every ski area I’ve been to has its pros and cons, something about Breck always keeps me personally wanting to go back. I’ve been a handful of times now and still feel like there is still more terrain that is safely approachable even if it is slightly outside of my current skill level. And the town is the perfect place to spend a day off the slopes.”

Breckenridge is bringing back its historic “Kingdom of Breckenridge” identity for 2026–’27, reviving a name rooted in the town’s independent spirit. Look for enhanced customer-first initiatives such as new rental delivery options and updated on-mountain dining choices.

A welcoming, family-friendly mountain with well-groomed runs, genuinely helpful staff, and a refreshing value proposition.

Making its debut in SKI’s Resort Guide rankings, Tamarack Resort offers a different kind of destination experience: approachable, relaxed, and refreshingly down-to-earth. Set in Idaho’s West Central Mountains above Lake Cascade, Tamarack wins points for the things that can make or break a family ski trip, from friendly staff who go out of their way to help to well-groomed runs that make cruising the mountain a pleasure. It’s not trying to be the flashiest resort in the West, and that’s part of its charm. “Skiing the way it should be,” said one survey respondent. “No crowds, stress-free powder days, amazing food, the best top-to-bottom groomers, and incredible views.”

Tamarack’s strengths are especially apparent for families and recreational skiers. Grooming helps keep the skiing approachable, while the welcoming atmosphere makes it easy and less intimidating for everyone to find their footing. The resort also scored well for value, making it an ideal target destination for budget-minded skiers. Here, the experience feels less about luxury trappings and more about making skiing fun and accessible. “Tamarack is like having your own private mountain,” shared a reader. “ For a small resort, it gives you a lot of bang for your buck!”

The trade-off is that Tamarack’s terrain may not satisfy skiers looking for endless variety or expert-level challenges. Strong intermediates and families can find plenty to enjoy, but advanced skiers seeking steep, technical terrain may want a bigger mountain with more options. And while Tamarack has the essentials for a mountain getaway, its amenities are more simple and down-home compared to some of the pricier destination resorts on this list.

But for skiers who value a friendly welcome, dependable grooming, and a more affordable family ski vacation over luxury and high-adrenaline terrain, Tamarack makes a compelling case. Its first appearance in the rankings reflects a resort that knows what it does well: an approachable mountain experience where the people can be just as memorable as the skiing. “It’s a hidden gem. Don’t tell anyone!”

“Tamarack has been my secret go-to resort for over 20 years. The mountain is great and it’s never crowded. I am often the only person I can see on a run. And now that the amenities are coming online it’s even better.”

Tamarack is joining Ikon Pass as a bonus mountain this winter. Ikon Pass holders get two free days at the resort. On the slopes, skiers can take advantage of free guided tours with mountain hosts and a refreshed rental fleet with new standard and demo skis. Off the slopes, the resort is adding seasonal experiences and events, including full-moon yoga, snowshoe and wine walks, and a keep an eye out for the Miracle Bar pop-up this winter.

5000-plus acres of bowls, glades, and cruisers meet a lively European-style village with world-class dining and après—if you can stomach the crowds and prices.

Vail has been a fixture in our Top 30 for good reason: Few resorts deliver such an expansive menu of terrain alongside a full-fledged destination experience. The mountain slipped several spots this year, with last winter’s disappointing snow conditions likely playing a role, but its core appeal remains intact. Across 5,280 skiable acres, there are the legendary Back Bowls, technical glades, wide-open cruisers, and enough variety to keep different ability levels happy for days. You can spend a morning chasing powder stashes and an afternoon cruising long blues without ever feeling like you’ve skied the same run twice. “Nothing compares to the Back Bowls, Shangri-La Glade on a powder day, or ending the day on Riva Ridge,” said one longtime Vail skier.

Another strength is the resort’s pedestrian village, with its Bavarian-inspired architecture. It’s one of the liveliest in Colorado, packed with lodging options, restaurants, bars, and places to keep the day going long after last chair. Readers continue to reward Vail for its dining, lodging, and après scene, which help make it a true vacation destination. Recent lift upgrades have also helped move skiers around the sprawling resort more efficiently.

Of course, Vail’s popularity comes at a price—literally. Lift tickets, lodging, meals, and drinks can add up quickly, and the crowds remain a sore point, especially on weekends and holidays. Parking is another familiar headache. Weekday visits can offer a more relaxed experience, but anyone planning a peak-period trip should expect to share the mountain with plenty of other skiers. The snow also needs to cooperate. In a lean season like last winter, even Vail’s massive terrain can’t always deliver the experience its reputation promises, but consensus was that mountain ops did a commendable job with what they got: “Groomers on the frontside were very well maintained and skiable despite the conditions,” said one skier.

Still, Vail remains one of Colorado’s most complete ski vacations. The combination of big-mountain variety, comfortable places to stay, excellent dining, and a village with real energy is hard to beat. It may not be the best choice for budget-conscious skiers or those who prize solitude, but for visitors looking to pair serious skiing with a memorable resort experience, Vail continues to deliver.

“Vail is the pinnacle of Colorado skiing. The most and best variety of terrain. High-speed lifts exactly where they should be. Powder that sticks around for days. And some of the best bars, shops, and restaurants of any ski town.”

Vail is doubling down on off-slope entertainment this winter. Dobson Arena debuts as a reimagined 56,000-square-foot music venue in the heart of the village, and pop-up mountaintop performances at Eagle’s Nest and other locations are in the plans. The resort’s Après All Day series is doubling in size, with more than 50 artists scheduled to perform on weekends throughout the winter.

On the mountain, Epic Ascent adds a concierge and priority lift-line access to private ski lessons, and dining updates range from a rebuilt signature burger to the return of the Ice Bar at the entrance to the Back Bowls.

A high-alpine playground with 360-degree summit skiing, deep snow, and a delightfully adventurous vibe—without the destination-resort price tag.

After a long hiatus, Mt. Bachelor is back in our rankings, and it brings a distinctly Pacific Northwest flavor to the list. This 9,065-foot volcano in Oregon’s Central Cascades is a fickle beast: One minute the skies are clear, the next wind and fog turn the summit into a Middle Earth landscape. But that wild, weather-dependent personality is part of the appeal. When conditions cooperate, skiers can explore 360 degrees of terrain off the summit, with wide-open alpine runs, natural wind lips, and playful features that make every lap feel a little different. “You can always find less-crowded terrain,” said one reader. “The layout is easy to navigate.”

Bachelor’s 4,300-plus acres offer fast groomers, side-piste stashes,  hemlock forests, and adventurous above-treeline terrain. “On a good snow year you can ski anywhere off the top of the mountain,” said a survey respondent. “There’s really no place quite like it!” The mountain earns high marks for its lifts, too, helping skiers make the most of the sprawling layout. Snow is another major draw: With annual snowfall topping 450 inches, Bachelor can stack up deep midwinter conditions, making it a favorite for powder seekers and tree-skiing devotees alike.

The resort also scores well for value and parking, a welcome combination in an era when a ski day can feel like a logistical and financial undertaking. Bachelor’s laid-back atmosphere puts the emphasis on skiing rather than resort trappings, and the mountain’s scale means there’s plenty to explore without the crowds and complications of some better-known destinations. Still, the experience has its trade-offs. There’s no lodging at the base, and on-mountain amenities are limited, so visitors looking for a full-service village best look elsewhere.

Fortunately, nearby Bend offers a lively home base, with breweries, restaurants, and places to stay. For skiers who value big terrain, deep snow, efficient lifts, and a little adventure over luxury, Bachelor is a worthy addition to the rankings—and a reminder that sometimes the best days come with a bit of wildness.

“Big mountain with excellent lift system, good snow, and grooming. With the base at 6,000 feet it has a long season and rarely seems to lack snow. I have skied here many times and it rarely seems to be crowded during the week.”

Mt. Bachelor is upgrading its Northwest Express chairlift with rebuilt top and bottom terminals, a new electric motor and diesel backup, and buried power and fiber lines designed to improve reliability during storms.

A laid-back local favorite with deep snow, playful terrain, and wallet-friendly prices, Brighton keeps things refreshingly unpretentious.

Tucked into Big Cottonwood Canyon alongside Solitude, this community-minded mountain wins skiers over with its welcoming, no-frills vibe, reliable terrain, and prices that feel refreshingly reasonable by Wasatch standards. It may not have the name recognition or polish of some of its neighbors, but Brighton has a loyal following and a character all its own. “I never really went to this resort but headed there with the lack of snow elsewhere and was amazed by the quality of skiing,” said one reader. “Skied here for a week and will be back next year!”

The snow is a major draw. Brighton’s position in the Wasatch makes it a compelling option for powder seekers, with terrain that rewards everyone from casual cruisers to skiers looking to duck into the trees. “Brighton had some of the best snow in the Wasatch last year,” shared one survey respondent. And with value among its top-scoring categories, the resort offers a relatively affordable way to tap into Utah’s famously deep snow. For visitors who care more about fresh turns than fancy amenities, Brighton can feel like a well-kept secret, even if it’s hardly undiscovered.

Of course, easy access has its downsides. Brighton’s proximity to Salt Lake City makes it convenient for locals and visitors alike, but canyon traffic, parking headaches, and crowds can complicate a powder day. The base area is modest, with limited dining and après options and little in the way of nightlife. Readers also gave the resort lower marks for dining and guest service, underscoring that Brighton’s appeal lies more in the skiing than the extras.

Still, Brighton’s climb to No. 22 reflects the appeal of simplicity: good snow, fun terrain, a friendly atmosphere, and an appealing price point. It may not be for skiers who want a bustling village or a full slate of amenities, but for those who measure a great day by the turns they make, Brighton delivers.

“Brighton is a great local choice that has really evolved over the last few years thanks to increased investment and attention from Boyne. Expanded night riding, the Crest 6, and a new mid-mountain lodge make for a great experience.”

Brighton celebrates its 90th anniversary this winter and is expanding night skiing to six nights a week. There are also new ticket options including an Off-Peak Pass and a Flex Pass for three, four, or five days of skiing. On the mountain, the Milly Chalet has been revamped as a quick-service Mexican restaurant serving tacos and margaritas.

A fiercely independent spirit, Southwestern soul, and some of the continent’s most challenging in-bounds terrain make Taos a mountain skiers fall hard for.

Taos climbed two spots this year despite the fact that last winter’s lean snow season hit the Southwest particularly hard—and that’s saying something. The Reader Resort Survey still shows what makes Taos special: a true sense of character, terrain that demands respect, and skiers who are eager to return year after year. Taos has an old-school spirit and a spicy Southwestern personality that’s unique to ski country.

Taos scored among its best in the challenge category, thanks to marquee steeps on Kachina Peak and hike-to terrain in West Basin. “I have skied here for over 50 years and am still discovering new lines and runs,” admitted one longtime skier. Yet Taos isn’t exclusively for experts. Its 1,294 acres are manageable enough for families to explore together, while traditional European-style Ski Weeks and the slopeside Blake Hotel lend the resort a distinctly alpine feel—with a New Mexico twist.

Taos also scored well for parking, an unsexy but practical perk that can make a ski day feel easier. The trade-off is a relatively small base village, with limited lodging and restaurant options compared with larger destinations. Après and grooming aren’t its strong suits, either. For a fuller taste of the region, make time for the town of Taos, where galleries, chile-forward cuisine, and the historic Taos Pueblo—a UNESCO World Heritage Site—offer plenty of reasons to stay off the mountain for a while. “Lots to do once you’re off the slopes and the New Mexico food is to die for!” said one survey respondent.

For skiers who value personality over polish, Taos has a way of getting under your skin. Its combination of serious terrain, independent character, and skier loyalty suggests that Taos’ devotees are in it for the long haul. When the snow cooperates, this mountain’s mix of steep lines, high-desert scenery, and Southwestern flavor is tough to replicate.

“Taos Ski Valley has kept its original charm. Definitely no corporate vibe here. When the snow is good, it’s a wonderful place.”

Taos has invested heavily in mountain improvements for 2026–’27, including additional glading, early-season snowmaking capabilities, and a satellite-based snow-depth mapping system to help crews groom more efficiently.

Big-mountain terrain, steep lines, and a spectacular Lake Tahoe backdrop make this a must-ski for experts willing to brave the crowds.

Palisades Tahoe is back in the rankings, and it’s easy to see why. Year after year, the resort earns high marks for challenge and terrain variety, two categories that matter most to skiers who like their mountains steep, technical, and a little intimidating. From legendary lines off KT-22 to big-mountain terrain that can keep even the strongest skiers busy, Palisades delivers the kind of skiing that reminds you why you fell in love with the sport in the first place. “More lift-served extreme terrain than most other places,” said one Palisades fan. “It’s incredible!”

One thing that sets Palisades apart from the rest is its proximity to Lake Tahoe, one of the most memorable backdrops in North American skiing. While the views aren’t quite as in-your-face as they are at Heavenly, they’re nothing to scoff at. And when the lifts stop spinning, Palisades has an après and nightlife scene with plenty of character, including iconic bars where swapping stories about the day’s lines is part of the ritual.

The biggest challenge is getting to the skiing itself. Parking reservations, crowded lots, and high parking fees remain sore points, especially on powder days when everyone seems to have the same idea. Reader also said that on-mountain dining can also use a boost, both in food quality and variety. And while Palisades can deliver unforgettable conditions, snow quality and grooming didn’t score as consistently well as its more celebrated terrain.

Still, for skiers who prize steep pitches and skiing in the tracks of the greats, Palisades Tahoe is worth the effort. Catch it on a storm day, and the combination of serious skiing and that Tahoe scenery can make for a bucket-list day on snow. “Great place to push your abilities if you want to, or just cruise away in the sunshine!”

“Great variety between the two mountains—legendary steeps at Palisades, excellent bowls and advanced terrain at Alpine. On the Alpine side you can find fresh tracks all day on a powder day.”

The resort is expanding snowmaking along Mountain Run and adding three diesel-electric snowcats equipped with technology that measures snow depth in an effort to provide a better early-season experience.

For learners, a new Progression Pass bundles three group lessons with lift tickets and rentals; skiers who complete all three lessons earn a season pass for the rest of the winter. Families can also take advantage of Kids Ski Free Week, Jan. 25–31, 2027, with free lift tickets for children and 20 percent off rentals, demos, and group lessons.

Impeccable grooming, attentive service, and family-friendly luxuries define this polished Rocky Mountain resort, where the skiing is excellent and the price of admission is steep.

The mountain offers plenty to keep skiers busy, even if its reputation for polished corduroy sometimes overshadows its more demanding terrain. Intermediates can cruise wide, beautifully maintained groomers, while families can explore the welcoming learning terrain at one of several beginner’s areas. On the other end of the spectrum, skiers can test themselves on sections of the Birds of Prey World Cup course when it’s not in use, or seek out the rocky, technical lines of the Stone Creek Chutes. The lifts keep things moving, and the resort’s attention to snow preparation makes even a routine groomer lap feel like a treat. “Surprisingly good options for advanced to expert skiers,” shared one Beaver Creek fan. “And since no one is seeking out that terrain, it’s never crowded.”

Off the slopes, Beaver Creek embraces its upscale identity. The pedestrian village, with its ice-skating rink, firepits, and Alps-inspired architecture, is designed for lingering, whether that means a leisurely dinner, a drink after skiing, or an afternoon with the kids. Dining and lodging are major strengths, with options that make it easy to turn a few days on the mountain into a full-fledged getaway. Families, in particular, benefit from the resort’s thoughtful touches, from its ski school to its signature Candy Cabin.

The trade-off is cost. Parking can be expensive and inconvenient, slopeside lodging comes at a premium, and dining bills climb quickly. Still, its relatively uncrowded slopes and consistently high level of service help justify the splurge for travelers who value a seamless ski vacation.

“There is Beaver Creek and then there is everywhere else. Excellent mountain for trees and moguls, which many people don’t seem to realize. Rarely crowded. Beautiful setting. Fine dining and lodging options.”

Beaver Creek is another recipient of Vail Resorts’ new focus on improved guest amenities, including Epic Ascent’s concierge service, premium gear rentals, and personalized on-mountain support. A new demo program adds high-performance options, including BOA ski boots. Keep an eye out for new mountain après experiences, including Last Drop Whiskey Tasting, Spruce Saddle Cabin Corner, and Boots & Bubbles at Mamie’s.

A Colorado local favorite with plenty of terrain to explore, reliable snow, and a no-frills spirit that shines brightest on the legendary bumps of Mary Jane.

Winter Park has a knack for being two things at once: an easy getaway for Denver skiers and a full-fledged mountain destination with enough terrain to keep visitors busy for days. The resort climbed one spot to No. 18 this year, earning high marks for terrain variety, challenge, access, and family friendliness.

The mountain’s breadth is a big part of the appeal. Beginners and families have plenty of room to progress, while stronger skiers can seek out hike-to lines off the Panoramic Express or the trees on Medicine Man. For groomer laps, Pioneer Express delivers, but the real heart of Winter Park for many regulars lies across the mountain at Mary Jane. Famous for its leg-burning mogul runs, Mary Jane has a personality all its own, from its challenging terrain to the tailgate scene that takes over the parking lot. “I love the variety of terrain, from easy cruisers to challenging double blacks,” said one survey respondent. “Enough terrain choices to match how you are feeling on any day.”

Winter Park’s proximity to Denver is both a blessing and a curse. It’s an accessible option for day-trippers, but busy weekends and holidays can mean crowded base areas and lift lines. Once you spread out across the mountain, though, there’s plenty of terrain to explore and pockets of breathing room to seek out. The Winter Park Express train, with service from Denver’s Union Station, offers another way to skip the highway grind, making the resort an appealing option for visitors without a car, too, which is not something many other Colorado resorts can offer.

The resort has invested in lodging and base-area amenities, but dining and character remain areas where it trails some destination competitors. Winter Park’s compact mountain village has some amenities, and there’s more dining and après down the road in the town of Winter Park. But for families and Front Range skiers who want a substantial day on snow without traveling deep into the Rockies, Winter Park remains a reliable favorite. “Great old-school vibe.”

“I love this resort. I love the layout, the terrain variety, and the size of the resort. I love the gladed skiing opportunities and the ability to pop into the trees and ski in more remote playful conditions when you want to get off busier groomed trails, while still feeling safe in the more isolated off-piste areas.”

Winter Park is adding more time on snow thanks to 8 a.m. weekend lift openings from Dec. 5 through April 4. The Rope Drop Club is also expanding, with eligible passholders who log early-season days invited to unlock Ski Patrol text alerts, early access to Mary Jane, and dedicated fast-track lanes at the Gondola and Super Gauge lifts.

A local favorite with naturally divided terrain, convenient access, and a no-frills Colorado ski vibe that puts the skiing ahead of the scene.

Copper Mountain climbed three spots this year, reinforcing its reputation as a dependable, down-to-earth ski destination for Front Range skiers. Just 75 miles west of Denver, Copper is easy to reach, easy to navigate, and refreshingly light on the glitz that defines some of Colorado’s bigger-name resorts. Its strong scores for value, lifts, and parking reflect the practical advantages that keep locals coming back: a solid mountain experience without quite as much hassle or expense.

One of Copper’s biggest assets is its naturally divided terrain. Beginners can stick to the gentle slopes on the western side, intermediates can cruise the blues in the middle, and experts can head east for steeper pitches and more demanding lines. That intuitive layout helps skiers find terrain suited to their ability without constantly crossing paths with people skiing at a different pace. The lift network makes getting around straightforward, too, giving skiers more time to rack up laps and less time figuring out where to go next. “Makes it easy to log a lot of ski time, particularly in the expert terrain areas,” opined one survey respondent.

Copper’s proximity to Denver means it’s hardly a secret, and weekends can get busy. Still, readers appreciate the free parking and shuttle service that connects the lots with the base areas. The resort feels more like a home mountain than a destination built around out-of-town visitors, with a loyal local crowd and a laid-back vibe that keeps the focus on skiing. For those who prioritize time on snow over a polished vacation experience, that’s part of the appeal.

Where Copper falls short is off the slopes. The base villages have limited dining and après options compared with those at more destination-minded resorts, and the nightlife is relatively subdued. Travelers looking for a lively mountain-town scene can head to nearby Frisco, just a few exits down I-70. But then, Copper’s appeal has never depended on fancy restaurants or late-night entertainment. It’s a place where you can still find free parking. ski hard, and get back out there the next morning. “Love the ease and convenience (and quiet nightlife) of the village and how easy it is to stay slopeside.”

“I’ve been skiing Copper my entire life (25-plus seasons) and no matter where else I ski, I keep coming back because it’s such a great resort. Easy to get around, good vibes, great terrain, the weather always seems magical.”

The Stifel Copper Cup Men’s Alpine World Cup takes place on Nov. 28–29 alongside a free O.A.R. concert. The resort is also expanding early-season snowmaking and debuting lowered season-pass prices, now with free skiing for kids ages 17 and under with the purchase of an adult pass.

Five-star service, family-friendly skiing, and a historic expansion are reshaping this polished Park City resort into a much bigger mountain experience.

Deer Valley has long made a compelling case for the luxury ski vacation, where impeccable service and thoughtful details make everything feel a little easier. From curbside drop-offs and complimentary ski valets to famously well-groomed slopes, the resort has built its reputation on taking the friction out of a ski day. Readers continued to reward that approach this year, giving Deer Valley high marks for guest service, family friendliness, dining, and lodging. “The service is the best I have ever experienced at a ski resort,” exclaimed one reader. Its convenient location near Salt Lake City International Airport is another major plus, making it one of the easier destination resorts to reach.

The biggest story, of course, is the mountain itself, which continues to get bigger. Deer Valley’s ambitious Expanded Excellence initiative has more than doubled its skiable terrain since December 2024, increasing the resort’s scale and opening up new terrain to explore. The expansion continues this season with the debut of Hail Peak Express in East Village, adding 200 skiable acres and seven new runs. The resort now boasts 4,500 skiable acres, 209 runs, and 32 lifts—a remarkable transformation for a destination once known as much for its carefully curated experience as its relatively compact footprint. The challenge will be maintaining the service, grooming, and sense of exclusivity that made Deer Valley famous as more skiers spread across the resort. “The new East side has tremendous potential!” said one skier.

Families remain especially well catered to, thanks to a great ski school, extensive ski-in/ski-out lodging, and thoughtful touches that make days on the slopes easier with kids. Dining is another standout, with more options than ever to choose from. Add in Park City’s restaurants, shops, and nightlife just down the road, and that’s a pretty well-rounded vacation experience.

Yet despite those strengths, Deer Valley fell from No. 9 to No. 16 in this year’s rankings, with value and challenge continuing to temper its overall appeal. Deer Valley is expensive, and skiers seeking the most demanding terrain may find its overall challenge less appealing than that of the competition. Still, with easy access from the airport, excellent family amenities, and thousands of new acres to explore, Deer Valley is entering a new era—one that could broaden its appeal beyond its traditional luxury niche.

“Snow making was taken to a new level this year. These guys worked miracles.”

The Hail Peak Express adds access to another 200 skiable acres and seven runs in East Village. Less exciting but still meaningful, snowmaking coverage will increase to 932 acres and East Village adds 1,200 day-skier parking spots.

A remote San Juan Mountains beauty with world-class terrain, a historic Western town, and the kind of all-in ski vacation that’s worth the effort to get there.

Telluride is not a spur-of-the-moment ski trip. Getting there takes planning, and once you factor in travel and lodging costs, it’s not exactly a bargain. But for skiers who make the journey, the payoff is a destination that delivers on nearly every front. Telluride slipped two spots to No. 15 in this year’s Resort Guide rankings, yet readers continued to reward its exceptional terrain variety, challenging skiing, and vibrant dining and lodging scene.

The San Juan Mountains provide a jaw-dropping backdrop for skiing that ranges from approachable groomers to some of Colorado’s most demanding terrain. Experts will find no shortage of ways to test themselves: Palmyra Peak, Gold Hill Chutes, and the hike-to terrain in Black Iron Bowl are just a few. Meanwhile, long blue cruisers deliver expansive views without requiring expert-level skills. Dedicated beginner terrain on its own section of the mountain gives newer skiers room to build confidence away from the chaos. “I tell everyone Telluride is my favorite, but tell them not to come so I can keep the place to myself,” admitted one reader.

Telluride’s historic Victorian mining town is a show-stopper, with independent shops, destination-worthy restaurants, a range of lodging, and a lively nightlife scene. Its laid-back Western personality feels a world away from the glossier, more commercialized ski villages, and the surrounding peaks are always in view. “Telluride is a magical town, with the resort right in the heart of it,” exclaimed one survey respondent. “Beautiful scenery, great runs. It’s hard to beat.”

The catch is getting there. Telluride’s small airport offers limited flights, while Montrose Regional Airport is roughly 90 minutes away by car. The drive from Denver takes about seven hours in good conditions, and the expense of reaching and staying in this remote corner of Colorado helps explain the resort’s low scores for access and value. But the distance also keeps casual traffic at bay, helping preserve the uncrowded feel and the sense that you’ve found somewhere truly special. Telluride may not be the easiest or cheapest trip to pull off, but for a bucket-list ski vacation, it’s tough to beat. “Best ski resort in Colorado …. maybe anywhere.”

“World-class terrain for all abilities and a cool old mining town like Aspen and Crested Butte.”

Book your stay: Outside+ members save up to 10% at SKI’s Top 50 Resorts. Find your dates.

Big-mountain challenge, a long season, and a refreshingly no-frills attitude keep this Colorado favorite true to its roots.

Arapahoe Basin is the type of place that inspires lifelong allegiance in its visitors. Readers call it “soulful,” praising its legit terrain, reliable snow, and refreshingly unpolished approach to the sport. This year, A-Basin climbed three spots to No. 14, earning its strongest marks for snow, challenge, value, and access.

Perched high on the Continental Divide, A-Basin packs a serious punch into a relatively compact footprint. Steep pitches and technical lines off the Pallavicini lift test expert skiers, while the hike-to chutes of the East Wall remain a bucket-list objective. But this isn’t a mountain reserved exclusively for the hardest-charging crowd. The Beavers and Montezuma Bowl cater to intermediates without sacrificing the rugged character that defines the place.

The value is another big part of the appeal. A-Basin delivers a high-alpine experience and an often exceptionally long season without the price tag of Colorado’s larger destination resorts. Its location makes it a relatively straightforward day trip from the Front Range. And then there’s the culture: the legendary Beach parking-lot scene, where tailgating is practically a sport, and spring traditions like Gay Basin Pride and the late-spring party scene give the mountain a personality all its own.

There’s no slopeside lodging, and anyone seeking a broad selection of restaurants, shopping, or perfectly manicured groomers would be best served looking elsewhere. But those shortcomings are part of the trade-off for a place that puts the skiing first. With a loyal following, challenging terrain, and a season that often stretches well beyond those of its neighbors, A-Basin remains one of Colorado’s most distinctive ski experiences. “It’s called The Legend for a reason.”

“As always, this is a skier’s mountain, there for skiers rather than developers. Even in a bad snow year like this one, the vibe is excellent.”

A-Basin celebrates its 80th anniversary with expanded Ikon Pass access: both Ikon Pass and Ikon Base Pass holders now receive unlimited access, replacing previous day limits.

A big-mountain experience with a small-town soul, Whitefish pairs dependable value and Montana snow with a ski town that hasn’t lost its independent streak.

Whitefish has had a bit of a roller-coaster ride in our Resort Guide rankings over the past five years, bouncing up and down the list as readers weigh in on everything from snow conditions to the overall experience. This year, the northern Montana favorite climbs from No. 22 to No. 13, buoyed by strong scores for value, snow, and local flavor. In an era when a ski vacation can feel like a luxury purchase, Whitefish continues to deliver something increasingly rare: a substantial mountain experience without the stratospheric prices. Shared one skier: “Huge, empty mountain, and there was plenty of snow when no one else had it.”

Located in northwest Montana, near the western entrance to Glacier National Park, Whitefish offers plenty of big-mountain skiing, with varied terrain, challenging pitches, and tree-lined runs that reward exploration. Its snowfall helped earn high marks this year, but the mountain’s appeal extends beyond the conditions. Whitefish has a laid-back, adventurous feel that favors getting after it on the slopes over putting on a show.

The town of Whitefish is every bit as much of a draw. Its walkable downtown, Western character, independent shops, and locally owned bars and restaurants give it a personality that can be hard to find in ski destinations increasingly cluttered with luxury developments. Readers appreciate the friendly locals and genuine sense of community, too. As the area grows in popularity, preserving that character will be key to keeping Whitefish special. For now, it remains a place where visitors can feel part of a real ski town rather than simply passing through a resort village.

Après may not be Whitefish’s strongest suit, but that’s a relatively small trade-off for skiers who value good snow, reasonable prices, and an authentic mountain experience. Its jump this year is a welcome rebound for a resort whose ranking has fluctuated considerably, and a reminder that Whitefish’s appeal lies as much in its character as in its skiing. “It’s stunning beauty on an inversion day. Heaven can wait!”

“This is a skier’s mountain. Plenty of groomers for those who like them, but thousands of acres of trees and bowls. Whitefish does more with limited snow cover than any resort I can name. With great cover and fresh snow, you can have fresh lines off-piste on every run.”

The resort cleared trees across roughly 200 acres near the Chair 1, Chair 2, and Chair 4 lift corridors to reduce wildfire risk and protect resort infrastructure. A happy byproduct of the work is two new tree runs, Bad Medicine and Big Twig, bringing the mountain’s total to 112 named trails.

Four mountains, one pass, and a world-class ski town make Aspen Snowmass a complete winter destination that delivers well beyond its glamorous reputation.

Four mountains, four distinct personalities, and some of the most varied terrain in North America: Aspen Snowmass has plenty going for it, from Aspen Mountain’s steep in-town runs to Snowmass’s sprawling groomers and Aspen Highlands’ hike-to challenges. “There is so much to love about Aspen—the quality and variety of terrain, the free ski shuttles, walkability of the town, and fantastic après scene, to name the most obvious,” said one survey respondent.

But even a destination with this much going for it isn’t immune to a tough snow year, which may have colored readers’ impressions this season and caused its drop in the rankings from No. 4 to No. 12. Add in the high cost of a ski vacation here and it’s easier to understand why the overall scores softened a bit.

The four diverse mountains are a big part of what makes the destination special. Aspen Mountain serves up steep, demanding runs right above downtown, while Buttermilk’s approachable terrain makes it a favorite for beginners and progression-minded skiers. Snowmass has an enormous range of terrain, from family-friendly groomers to serious steeps, and Aspen Highlands draws experts with its formidable vertical and hike-to adventures. Recent terrain expansions, including the Hero’s addition of 150 acres of steep chutes and trees on Aspen Mountain, give strong skiers even more reason to explore.

Off the slopes, Aspen Snowmass really separates itself from destinations that are all skiing and little else. Readers gave it high marks for lifts, dining, lodging, après, and nightlife, and the options are as varied as the terrain: think lively mountain bars, destination-worthy restaurants, luxury hotels, and intimate inns. Aspen’s polished reputation can make it seem like a playground exclusively for the wealthy, and there’s no pretending this is a budget destination. But the broader experience is more accessible than its reputation suggests, with plenty of ways to enjoy the mountains and town without going full celebrity mode. “I love the beautiful scenery and the overall experience of the resort, including the fast lifts and the plentiful food and drink options,” shared an Aspen Snowmass skier. “The vibe is just awesome, simply put.”

The biggest hurdles remain access and cost. The drive from Denver can stretch beyond four hours in winter weather, and flights into Aspen’s small airport can be expensive and limited. Once you arrive, lift tickets, lodging, dining, and après can quickly add up. Still, for skiers looking for a destination where exceptional skiing meets a vibrant mountain town, Aspen Snowmass remains hard to beat.

“The Aspen skiing complex is one of the best in North America; with a wide variety of skiing situated among four distinct mountains, you can take your pick.  On-mountain eating may be pricey, but the food is fantastic and carefully prepared.”

Snowmass’s beloved Ullrhof restaurant returns after a complete rebuild, with seating for more than 600, a new bar and lounge level, and Aspen Snowmass’s first fully electric kitchen design. On Aspen Mountain, the new Nell Bell lift makes its debut this winter. Also, a new Flex Pass offers four to seven days of access across all four mountains, giving travelers more flexibility to work Aspen Snowmass into a longer winter trip without committing to an Ikon Pass.

Home to some of Utah’s best snow and most demanding terrain, Snowbird inspires fierce loyalty among skiers who would rather chase powder than polish their après game.

Snowbird climbed three spots this year, reaffirming its status as one of North America’s premier destinations for serious skiers. The secret isn’t much of a secret: Little Cottonwood Canyon gets absolutely hammered by storms, delivering the kind of light, dry powder that keeps Snowbird’s devoted following coming back for more. And when the snow piles up, the mountain’s steep, technical terrain offers endless ways to put it to work.

From the narrow chutes and cliff bands of Gad Valley to the wide-open powder stashes of Mineral Basin, The Bird rewards skiers who like their runs challenging and their days untracked. Organ Grinder delivers steep, tree-lined turns, while the mountain’s formidable vertical keeps even the strongest legs honest. It’s no surprise Snowbird landed in the Top 5 for both snow and challenge in this year’s reader survey. “Snowbird makes me a better skier,” shared one of the resort’s many fans. “I have fun even when I’m pushing myself past my comfort zone.”

The culture is part of the appeal, too. Snowbird has a refreshingly down-to-earth attitude, where strangers bond over shared powder stashes. But getting to that legendary terrain can be a challenge of its own. “It’s more no-frills than a lot of places but it doesn’t feel old school.”

Readers continue to gripe about traffic in crowded Little Cottonwood Canyon and the headache of finding a parking spot, particularly on powder days. Grooming and lifts also remain sore spots. Still, for skiers willing to navigate the access hassles, the payoff is often worth the trouble.

“Great snow and great skiing. Spring skiing in Mineral Basin can’t be beat. Top to bottom from the tram is superb. Ski Patrol is always awesome, super friendly, and helpful.”

Snowbird opened The Nest, a new restaurant, late last year, adding a fresh dining option to the resort. The mountain also introduced the Chickadee chairlift and expanded its beginner terrain, helping to broaden its appeal beyond the steep-and-deep crowd.

Legendarily steep terrain, Tetons-for-days scenery, and a cowboy-chic town where big-mountain adventure meets luxury.

Some mountains are built for cruising. Not Jackson Hole. This place is all about pushing your limits—and then celebrating your survival over a waffle at Corbet’s Cabin. Back in the Top 10 this year, the Wyoming icon remains one of North America’s ultimate proving grounds for big-mountain skiers, earning a No. 2 ranking for terrain challenge and Top 10 marks for both snow and après. The scenery is spectacular, the skiing can be downright intimidating, and the experience is unmistakably Jackson.

The iconic Aerial Tram is practically a rite of passage, delivering skiers to the high-alpine terrain that has made this mountain famous. From steep chutes and technical tree skiing to cliffs, couloirs, and the legendary Corbet’s Couloir, Jackson rewards those who come prepared to get after it. And with so much terrain to explore, even experienced skiers can spend a lifetime finding new lines. “It’s not for the faint of heart,” advised one reader. “True advanced and expert skiers will enjoy it the most.” The surrounding backcountry adds another layer of adventure for qualified skiers with the right equipment, knowledge, and conditions.

But you don’t need to be a full-time adrenaline junkie to enjoy Jackson. The resort has broadened its appeal in recent years, with more approachable terrain giving intermediates room to build confidence and rack up laps. Après Vous is a go-to zone for green and blue cruisers, while Solitude Station offers a convenient mid-mountain hub. It’s still a mountain with a serious reputation, but there’s more to it than cliff drops and steep lines that make your knees wobble.

Off the slopes, the experience is just as memorable. Twelve miles down the road, the town of Jackson blends its Western roots with upscale hotels, excellent restaurants, lively bars, and plenty of places to toast a big day. Après is a strength, and the combination of rugged mountain adventure and polished town comforts is a big part of the appeal. The downside? Getting here takes some planning, and a Jackson vacation can be expensive. Readers also see room for improvement in guest service, a reminder that a world-class mountain doesn’t automatically make every part of the experience world-class.

Still, Jackson Hole has a way of getting under your skin. It’s wild without being remote, luxurious without sacrificing its Western vibe and challenging enough to keep even the strongest skiers humble. Its return to the Top 10 reflects what readers already know: Few places deliver this particular mix of big-mountain skiing, deep snow, and Western personality. “Lives up to its expert reputation. Love the cowboy vibe!”

“It’s hard to beat JHMR for big terrain. The sidecountry is incredible, and even inbounds you can feel like you’re in a ski movie. Great snow and amazing views.”

Getting to Jackson Hole is easier thanks to expanded nonstop service from 14 cities, including a new route from Boston. Skiers can also show their boarding pass for a complimentary afternoon lift ticket, subject to availability. And for planning purposes, note that Ikon and Mountain Collective passholders will again need lift reservations, with daily mountain capacity managed by the resort.

Deep powder, easy parking, and a down-to-earth mountain vibe that’s getting harder to find.

Tucked on the western side of the Tetons, Grand Targhee is the kind of place skiers whisper about when they want to share a great find without blowing the whole place up. With more than 2,600 skiable acres and a reputation for reliably deep snow, it delivers the goods without the crowds, complications, or high-gloss resort scene that have become standard elsewhere. “I have been skiing Targhee for 20 years,” shared one Targhee fan. “It’s a treasure that makes me feel I have gone 25 years back in time—laidback, friendly, and what skiing in the U.S. used to be.”

Teton storms regularly roll through, piling light, dry snow onto Targhee’s west-facing slopes. When the flakes fly, the mountain’s wide-open bowls, spacious groomers, and well-spaced trees are primed for everything from playful powder laps to full-on face shots. The terrain may not match the sheer scale or intensity of nearby Jackson Hole, but there’s plenty to keep adventurous skiers entertained, such as Pat’s Face and the triple-black glades off the Dreamcatcher chair. And with snow ranking among the resort’s strongest attributes in our survey, your odds of finding something soft to ski are a big part of the draw.

Then there’s the experience off the slopes—or, more accurately, the lack of fuss surrounding it. Parking is refreshingly manageable, the atmosphere is welcoming, and families can focus on skiing instead of navigating a complicated resort operation. Employees are genuinely friendly, locals are happy to share their favorite stashes, and nobody seems particularly interested in impressing anyone. Grand Targhee’s value is another major selling point: You’re here for the snow and the skiing, not for a designer village or a scene that requires a reservation.

The base area keeps things simple, with a handful of lodging and dining options and the ever-popular Trap Bar for post-ski beers and stories. For more restaurants, coffee shops, and après options, head down the road to Driggs, Idaho, a growing mountain town with plenty of independent character. Lodging and nightlife aren’t Targhee’s strongest suits, and anyone looking for a sprawling resort village may come away underwhelmed. But that’s also the point. This is a place where the mountain takes center stage, and the rest is refreshingly uncomplicated. “The vibe is quaint yet the slopes are expansive,” summed up one survey respondent.

“I loved the way the resort was laid out. It’s really easy to understand the trail map and navigate the various areas.”

Two enormous mountains, world-class alpine terrain, and a village that knows how to keep the party going.

Whistler Blackcomb is the kind of place that makes you want to extend your trip before the end of your first day. This sprawling British Columbia megaresort ranked No. 1 for terrain variety in our survey for good reason: Between the two mountains, skiers can spend days bouncing between wide-open alpine bowls, steep chutes, glades, glaciers, and cruisers that let you catch your breath before doing it all again. Whether you’re chasing technical lines or simply want to explore a mountain that seems to go on forever, Whistler delivers big-mountain skiing with an international flavor few resorts can match. “Loved the vastness of Whistler.,” said one survey respondent. “Went with a group; every level of skier was happy.”

That international spirit extends straight into the pedestrian village, where après turns into dinner, dinner turns into drinks, and so on. The restaurants, boutiques, bars, and hotels draw visitors from across the globe, creating an energy that’s hard to replicate at a more isolated ski area. Readers ranked Whistler No. 1 for après, dining, and lodging, confirming that the experience here is about much more than clocking vertical. When the lifts close, the fun is only getting started. As one reader put it: “Whistler has the best après out of any resort in North America, hands down.”

Whistler’s popularity means crowded slopes and long lift lines, particularly during peak periods, while high prices can make a ski vacation here a serious investment. Readers also called out grooming and on-mountain food as areas where the experience doesn’t always live up to the mountain’s reputation. With terrain this extensive, conditions can vary considerably across the resort, and the sheer number of visitors can make finding your own patch of quiet snow a challenge. Still, for many skiers, those are the trade-offs that come with skiing one of the world’s great mountain destinations.

Whistler Blackcomb’s appeal is the sheer range of experiences packed into one place. You can spend the morning exploring high-alpine terrain, refuel on a sunny patio, and finish the day swapping stories over cocktails without ever needing to get in a car. This year, readers nudged the resort up to No. 8, a reminder that the combination of serious skiing and a seriously good time remains tough to beat. Just bring your legs, your appetite, and a realistic budget.

“Grew up here. Keep trying to find other resorts, but I keep coming back.”

The new Showcase Chair, a fixed-grip quad replacing the Showcase T-Bar, makes for easier access to Blackcomb’s high-alpine terrain. Whistler Blackcomb is also investing in its on-mountain dining: Dusty’s brings back breakfast, while Pika’s at the Roundhouse gets a renovation that includes a new coffee bar and an elevated comfort-food menu. At Steeps Grill & Wine Bar on Whistler Mountain and Christine’s on Blackcomb, enhanced outdoor patios add lounge seating, fire pits, and more reasons to linger over lunch.
