Big Sierra storms, more than 3,500 acres of wildly varied terrain, and a ski-town vibe that runs on pure stoke.

Mammoth Mountain embodies the best of California ski culture: a big mountain, big storms, and a laidback vibe that makes skiing here feel different than anywhere else in North America. Tucked into the eastern Sierra Nevada, Mammoth has steadily earned its place among the West’s most beloved ski destinations, thanks to more than 3,500 acres of terrain and a refreshingly unpretentious ambience.

The terrain is as varied as it is expansive. Cruise wide-open groomers, hunt for wind-buffed powder in the bowls, or point your tips down steep, technical pitches that demand your full attention. The lift network makes it possible to cover serious ground, and on those bluebird days after a big storm, the mountain can feel almost limitless. “Mammoth lives up to its name,” agreed one reader. “The place is HUGE.” Mammoth’s famously long season is another draw: When plenty of other resorts have called it quits, this mountain often still has plenty of skiing left.

Of course, those legendary storms come with a catch. Sierra weather can be wild, and high winds frequently put upper-mountain lifts on hold. Knowing where to go—and when to pivot—is part of the Mammoth learning curve. The upside is that when the wind finally settles and the upper mountain opens, the payoff can be spectacular.

Getting here requires some commitment. Mammoth sits more than 300 miles from Los Angeles, making it a haul for Southern California weekend warriors and a serious road trip for just about everyone else. But that distance helps preserve the mountain’s independent spirit. Down in Mammoth Lakes, the vibe is more practical than polished, with a mix of craft breweries, restaurants, and places to refuel after a day on the hill. It may not be the most picturesque ski town in the West, but it has the essentials.

Mammoth isn’t for skiers who need every detail of a vacation to run like clockwork. But for those willing to roll with it, the reward is a big, unapologetically Californian mountain with terrain for nearly every ability and a culture that needs no reminder that this sport is supposed to be fun. Keep the expectations flexible, bring your stoke, and Mammoth can deliver the kind of days that keep skiers coming back. “Just a great mountain with terrain options for every taste. Great SoCal vibe.”

“I’ve been skiing there for 10 years now and everytime I go up, I experience something new.  If you want steeps, they’ve got that. If you want long groomers, they have those. Beginner terrain, there are multiple areas on the mountain to choose from. With the way the wind blows at Mammoth, it may not have snowed in three or four days but the wind buff creates wonderful stashes of powder. Basically, If you aren’t having a great time skiing at Mammoth, you’re not trying.”

Silky corduroy, fall-line steeps, and a real Western ski town that knows a thing or two about doing winter right.

Sun Valley has been winning over skiers for generations, and it’s not about to start chasing anyone else’s idea of a great ski resort. A longtime fixture in SKI’s rankings, the Idaho classic climbs from No. 8 to No. 6 this year on the strength of what it has always done exceptionally well: fast, impeccably groomed skiing, an efficient lift network, and service that makes the whole experience feel effortless. From the moment you pull up to the storied Sun Valley Lodge to your first carve down a freshly groomed run on Bald Mountain, there’s a sense that someone here has thought of everything. Consider it old-school hospitality with very good skiing attached.

Baldy, as the locals call it, is the main event. Its sustained fall-line pitches are tailor-made for skiers who like to lay over big, satisfying turns on perfectly manicured corduroy. The grooming is among the best around, and modern lifts make it easy to rack up laps without wasting half the morning in line. Families and newer skiers can head to Dollar Mountain, where approachable terrain offers room to build confidence. The trade-off? Sun Valley doesn’t have the sprawling mix of terrain found at some larger Western resorts, and natural snowfall can be less reliable than at its snowier neighbors. But the mountain operations team has earned its reputation for making the most of what falls from the sky. “Excellent snowmaking and grooming,” opined one Sun Valley skier. “The resort maintained terrific conditions—far better than any other U.S. resort—despite the lack of snow this season.”

Just down the road, Ketchum delivers the sort of genuine mountain-town character that can be hard to find amid the condos and carefully curated villages of newer resorts. Locals and visitors mingle over après drinks at the mix of trendier bars and well-established watering holes. The airport in nearby Hailey makes getting here easier than you might expect, with service from four major airlines connecting the area to eight U.S. cities. It’s still a trip that takes some planning, but the payoff is a ski vacation with a distinctive sense of place. “Sun Valley is magical. The town of Ketchum is a perfect ski town,” said one reader.

“Sun Valley always provides an exceptional experience. Their snowmaking and grooming is impeccable, always. While there may not have been much snow out West this season, I consistently skied over 50k vertical days! The village, restaurants, and Ketchum as a whole is a skiers paradise!”

Sun Valley is replacing two lifts this winter: Lookout Express will be upgraded to a detachable six-person lift and the Christmas fixed-grip quad will become a high-speed quad. They also added 50 acres of glades on Bald Mountain.

Sun Valley has also earned a first-in-the-nation DarkSky certification, recognizing its commitment to protecting the naturally dark night skies that make this corner of Idaho special.

Finally, for ski-racing fans, the Audi FIS Ski World Cup Finals return to Bald Mountain on March 19–25, bringing the world’s best racers to one of the sport’s most storied American venues.

A mountain of epic proportions, a lift network built for maximum laps, and enough Lone Peak bragging rights to last all winter.

Big Sky may have relinquished its No. 1 crown, but dropping to No. 5 is hardly a fall from grace. This is still one of North America’s most impressive ski experiences, with a seemingly endless supply of terrain, wide-open spaces, and a lift network that makes getting around a whole lot easier than it used to be. In southwestern Montana, Big Sky does everything on a big scale—and largely delivers. “When it comes to terrain, infrastructure, snow quality and natural splendeur, Big Sky is the real deal!”

The headliner is Lone Peak, whose 11,166-foot summit serves up steep chutes, exposed lines, and big-mountain terrain. But you don’t have to be a send-it-all-the-time expert to appreciate the place. Intermediates can rack up miles on rolling groomers and wide-open trails, while beginners have room to find their feet without feeling swallowed by the mountain. With so much terrain spread across the resort, there’s always another zone to explore and another reason to come back.

Boyne Resorts has spent years making that sprawling playground easier to ski, and the results are hard to miss. Heated high-speed lifts, including the eight-person Madison 8, keep the laps coming, while the Lone Peak Tram delivers skiers to the summit in less than four minutes. The new-last-season Explorer Gondola is the latest upgrade, connecting Mountain Village and the upper mountain, making it easier to spend more time skiing. It hasn’t gone unnoticed: “Big Sky has spent an extensive amount of money on new lifts,” commented one SKI reader. “Trams, gondolas, six- and eight-person bubble heated seat chairs. By far the closest to the European experience.”

Of course, skiing at this scale comes with a price tag to match. Readers dinged Big Sky for expensive lodging, on-mountain dining, and add-on costs, including parking and the tram ride to Lone Peak. The village could also use a little more après-ski energy once the lifts stop spinning. Town Center, a couple of miles down the road, offers more options for dinner and drinks, but Big Sky isn’t exactly the place to book if your ideal ski vacation involves dancing until last call. Then again, when the mountain is this good, an early bedtime and first chair might be just what the body needs.

“I skied there for four days and can ski another 40 and never get bored. Endless double and triple black lines off Lone Peak.  More and better expert terrain than anywhere I have seen in the U.S., but also fabulous terrain variety.”

It’s the first full winter for the 10-person Explorer Gondola and Kircliff Observatory. The gondola connects the base village with the Lone Peak Tram, and Kercliff, located at 11,166 feet, delivers sweeping 360-views across three states and two national parks.

Nearly 550 inches of snow a year, terrain worthy of its cult following, and a refreshingly old-school approach to skiing.

Some ski resorts sell a lifestyle. Alta sells skiing—and lots of it. Heading into its 89th season, this Little Cottonwood Canyon classic has climbed from No. 6 to No. 4 in our rankings, a reminder that you don’t need a flashy village or a thrilling après lineup to win over skiers. Alta is unapologetically skier-centric, resolutely low-key, and gloriously uninterested in keeping up with the Joneses. “A true skiers’ paradise.”

The snow helps. A lot. Alta earned the top spot in our snow category, thanks to nearly 550 inches of famously light Utah powder in an average season. When a storm rolls through, the mountain’s bowls, chutes, and steep pitches deliver the kind of deep, floaty turns that can make even a seasoned skier feel like a convert. “It’s powder heaven,” says one cult of Alta member. “All the best skiing days of my life have been at Alta.” But unlike some hardcore-favorite mountains, Alta isn’t exclusively for experts. There’s plenty of intermediate terrain, too, even if the steep-and-deep stuff tends to hog the spotlight.

The experience feels refreshingly old-school, from the modest base village to the emphasis on skiing over spectacle. Lodging and dining are available right at the mountain, and the overall cost can compare favorably with flashier destination resorts, particularly if you value time on snow over a resort-town nightlife scene. Families who appreciate a simpler ski vacation may find plenty to love here, though the lack of non-skiing diversions and the mountain’s skiers-only policy mean Alta isn’t everyone’s cup of cocoa.

Getting there can be the rub. Little Cottonwood Canyon traffic is notoriously challenging on powder days, weekends, and holidays, and a storm can turn the trip uphill into an exercise in patience. Staying in one of Alta’s village lodges can spare you some of the daily commute, but for everyone else, an early start and a flexible attitude are wise. Of course, once you’re floating through fresh snow, the traffic-induced grumbling tends to fade. That’s the Alta effect: You swear you’ll never sit in that canyon traffic again, then wake up the next morning and do it all over again. “When I die, you can bury me at Alta.”

“The terrain is phenomenal, but it’s not for everybody. It’s definitely a vibe.”

The multiyear renovation and expansion of Albion Lodge is complete, including the new Albion Grill with expanded patio seating. There’s also more opportunity for women to build skills and confidence with an expanded lineup of women’s camps, clinics, and lessons, led by experienced female instructors.

World-class lifts, seriously good food, and big-mountain skiing just 45 minutes from Salt Lake City. No wonder skiers keep coming back.

Snowbasin has been a reader favorite for years, and they’re not letting up: The Wasatch resort holds steady at No. 3 for 2027. Just 45 minutes from Salt Lake City, Snowbasin delivers big-mountain skiing with an unusually smooth experience from the parking lot to the last chair. That combination of easy access and painless parking is no small feat in Utah.

What really sets Snowbasin apart, though, is how well it does just about everything. The lifts are fast, the grooming is dialed, the guest service is excellent, and the on-mountain dining is a cut above the rest. The grand lodges feel more like mountain retreats than places to grab a quick lunch, and the terrain offers something for just about every kind of skier. Cruise the wide-open groomers off the DeMoisy Express, or head for the bowls, steeps, and cornices around Needles Cirque when you’re ready to get your heart rate up. It’s a mountain that can keep a mixed-ability group happy without anyone feeling like they’re compromising.

That polished experience extends to the people behind it. Snowbasin consistently earns high marks for guest service, and its dining options deliver both variety and quality. In other words, the mountain knows that a great ski day involves more than vertical feet. You’ll find plenty of places to refuel in style, whether you’re settling in for a leisurely lunch or just warming up between laps. “Everything is sublime!” said one happy Snowbasin skier. “The bathrooms are top-tier and the food is scrumptious, great gluten-free options compared to other resorts.”

There is one notable trade-off: No lodging at the base means Snowbasin isn’t a true destination resort in the traditional sense. And if you’re looking for a lively après scene right outside the lifts, you’ll need to look elsewhere. Luckily, nearby Ogden offers a range of places to stay, eat, and unwind, while Salt Lake City puts even more options within reach.

The bigger challenge these days is that the secret is decidedly out. Snowbasin has become a popular alternative when Little Cottonwood Canyon closes. With the resort slated to host alpine racing at the 2034 Winter Olympics, that spotlight will only grow brighter. The upside for skiers? Continued investment in lifts, terrain, and infrastructure that should make an already excellent mountain even better. “They’ve invested heavily in the lift infrastructure—the new Becker lift this season was a tremendous improvement and makes getting over to Strawberry so easy,” shared one survey respondent.

“Even in a low-snow winter, Snowbasin shined with a great experience. Their staff is the best in the industry, and the amenities are unbeatable. If you’re stuck on groomed trails, there really isn’t a better place. But on a powder day, this place really performs.”

Big-mountain terrain, jaw-dropping scenery, and a ski trip that won’t break the bank. What’s not to love?

A jump from No. 15 to No. 2 is no small feat, but Lake Louise has plenty going for it. Start with the value: Thanks to the favorable Canadian dollar, your ski-trip budget goes further here. Then there’s the skiing. Set within the spectacular wilderness of Banff National Park, Lake Louise serves up 4,200 acres of terrain with a distinctly Canadian Rockies flavor. Fast, confidence-building groomers? Check. Steep, rugged lines on the backside? Absolutely. Wide-open bowls, tree skiing, and enough variety to keep even seasoned skiers exploring? You bet. The scenery is almost unfairly good, too. As one reader put it, “Fantastic steep and deep chute skiing. No lines and amazing snow. Can’t be beat.”

The resort’s latest terrain additions make an already sprawling mountain even easier to explore. New connections link key areas of the mountain, while expanded glades give intermediate and advanced skiers more options in the trees. Snowmaking improvements on Richardson’s Ridge should also help deliver more consistent conditions when Mother Nature needs a little backup.

All of that said, you won’t find slopeside lodging or much in the way of après at the ski area itself. For that, head to Banff, a lively mountain town about 35 miles away with plenty of restaurants, bars, shops, and places to stay. Free, regular shuttles connect the town to the resort, so a rental car isn’t a must. And while Lake Louise may not have the buzzy village scene of some destination resorts, that’s part of its appeal: You come here for the mountain, the wilderness, and the kind of big-skiing experience that still feels refreshingly unpretentious. As one aficionado summed it up: “Lake Louise is simply the greatest. Love the mountain. Love Banff.”

“Lots of snow this year. I had some amazing powder days at Louise. The back bowls are some of my favorite areas to ski as well as the West Bowl. Even on non powder days, I enjoyed cruising in the alpine on the frontside.”

The resort adds Grove Glade, a groomed green run linking Merlin’s directly to the new Richardson’s Ridge chairlift, while Pikaboo and Prunepicker improve connections between key zones. Expanded intermediate and advanced glades offer even more tree-skiing options.

The resort is also upgrading the Top of the World chairlift to improve loading capacity, speed, and reliability, and expanding snowmaking coverage on Richardson’s Ridge for more consistent conditions.

A high-alpine playground with naturally reliable snow, fast lifts, and jaw-dropping Canadian Rockies views. Now, for the first time, it’s North America’s favorite ski resort.

There’s a reason skiers keep coming back to Banff Sunshine. Actually, there are several: big-mountain scenery, reliably good snow, a long season, and terrain that ranges from mellow groomers to legitimately intimidating steeps. “High elevation means great quality and plentiful snow throughout the very long season,” wrote one Sunshine fan. “Always finding new fun spots even though I’ve been skiing here for years.”

And then there’s the setting. Just 15 minutes from the lively town of Banff, Sunshine feels like a world apart once you’re riding the gondola up through the trees toward its sprawling, above-treeline playground. It’s the kind of place that reminds you why you ski in the first place.

This year, our readers put Banff Sunshine at the top of North America’s 50 best resorts for the first time ever, bumping it up from last year’s No. 2 ranking. It makes sense. Sunshine has invested in its lift system in recent years, making it easier to explore the mountain and a whole lot more comfortable to ride on blustery days. Bubble enclosures on select lifts are a particularly welcome touch at a resort where exposed alpine terrain is part of the appeal—and where the weather can be as big as the views.

The skiing itself offers plenty of ways to spend a day. Cruise rolling groomers, roam across wide-open bowls, or work your way toward the steeper, more technical terrain. For experts, Delirium Dive is talked up a lot, but don’t overlook some other sleepers, including Stampede and Saddledome on Goat’s Eye Mountain. Sunshine may not have the sheer variety of some larger North American resorts, but its mix of approachable laps and high-alpine challenges makes it an easy place for a group with different abilities to enjoy skiing together.

Then there’s the snow. With a summit just shy of 9,000 feet and no snowmaking, Sunshine depends entirely on Mother Nature—and has the elevation and climate to make the most of it. Its season regularly stretches into late May, giving skiers a shot at spring turns long after many other resorts have called it a year. That extended season, combined with the big-mountain setting, is a pretty compelling argument for making the trip. “Banff is my favorite place to ski,” opined one survey respondent. “The mountain is beautiful, the terrain is varied, and it’s not overcrowded. The town of Banff is perfect. Like a postcard. Most beautiful place in North America.”

The one catch? You can’t exactly roll out of bed and onto the lifts from a sprawling slopeside resort village. Sunshine Mountain Lodge is the ski area’s only overnight accommodation, and scoring a room means waking up to a rare kind of quiet, with first tracks practically on your doorstep. Everyone else can stay in Banff, where free shuttles make getting to the mountain refreshingly simple. The town has everything from affordable hotels to more upscale digs, plus enough restaurants, bars, bowling alleys, and other diversions to fill an evening—or several. The combination of wild alpine skiing by day and a proper mountain town by night is hard to beat.

What’s New The ski area is amping up its après offerings this season. The GOAT Club returns to Goat’s Eye Lodge for a second season of live music, DJs, and après parties, and the new Coco Lounge, on the deck of the Centennial Lodge, will serve cocktails and food with fireside seating and slope views. If you need a warm-up between laps,  Coco Lab Coffee & Hot Chocolate Bar is now at Goat’s Eye Lodge serving hot drinks. There’s also a new cocktail bar if you want something a little stronger.

OpenAI releases GPT-6 Cyber, and promises this one stays within 'rules of engagement'

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The latest bug hunting model 'avoided exploiting out-of-scope services over 99 percent of the time', claims House of Altman

OpenAI, on Thursday, released its latest cybersecurity-tuned model, GPT-6 Cyber, which the House of Altman says combines "stronger cybersecurity capabilities with better adherence to researchers’ rules of engagement."

The new model, we’re told, is much better at finding and exploiting vulnerabilities, compared to GPT-5.6 and GPT-6 Astra.

OpenAI released GPT-6 Astra in early September, and less than a month later, killed off the planned release of GPT-6.1 Astra after the model got better at pursuing tasks but worse at staying within the boundaries of what it had actually been authorized to do.

The model maker said the latest cyber model was "trained to stay within authorized scope, respect restrictions and report results accurately."

In OpenAI's ExploitGym training evaluation, GPT-6 Cyber scored 48.5 percent. For comparison, GPT-5.6 Cyber scored 32.1 percent on these same benchmark tests.

"In a separate evaluation of whether the model respects the boundaries of an assigned task, it avoided exploiting out-of-scope services over 99 percent of the time," Open AI said. Only 0.07 percent of cases went beyond the intended scope of the training exercise.

OpenAI declined to provide additional details about what happened in that 0.07 percent of cases and whether they involved any real-life safety or security incidents - like hacking companies or carrying out supply chain attacks as its earlier models have done during training. We do know, however, that OpenAI recently began denying internet access to the frontier models it is training to help prevent its models from doing bad things.

The latest cyber model is also really good at finding and exploiting serious security vulnerabilities, OpenAI claimed. Its researchers used GPT-6 Cyber to find 81 previously unknown high-severity vulnerabilities in Firefox, which Mozilla has started fixing. The model also helped researchers develop working exploits for 948 Linux kernel vulnerabilities, including 588 previously unknown bugs. The Linux findings are being reported with potential patches for maintainer review.

OpenAI classified GPT-6 Cyber as “Critical” under its Preparedness Framework. This is its highest-risk tier for cyber capabilities, and means a model can both identify and develop functional zero-day exploits in real-world critical systems, and execute end-to-end cyberattacks.

As such, it’s initially only available through the API and Codex to a select group of approved Daybreak Red customers - these are customers who are authorized to use OpenAI’s models for offensive security actions such as proof-of-concept exploit development, exploit-chain validation, penetration testing, and red teaming.

Over the coming weeks, OpenAI will expand access to all Daybreak Red customers, and is also making GPT-5.6 Cyber available to this group, as well.

The model release comes as OpenAI makes a concerted push to improve the security of its products.

Earlier this week, OpenAI raised its bug bounty reward payments, and added new bonuses for independent researchers.

This includes quadrupling reward-range midpoints, on average, for medium, high and critical findings, so a critical vulnerability in a core product such as ChatGPT, Codex, or the API Platform will now earn up to $30,000 - or up to or $51,577 after bonuses - compared to the previous $6,500 payout.

Exceptional critical findings on those targets remain eligible for up to $100,000, and now up to $172,000 with bonuses.®

Inside the persistent myth that giant pandas are bad at sex

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 5, 2026 5:00 PM EST

Excerpted from INTHE COMPANY OF PANDAS: My Life with the World’s Favorite Bear by Dr. Brandie Smith. Copyright 2026 © by Dr. Brandie Smith. Reprinted by permission of Atria Books, an Imprint of Simon & Schuster, LLC.

WHEN YOU take care of pandas, your primary responsibility is to make more pandas. I was told point-blank during my interviews to become curator, “Your job is to get the panda pregnant.” I had a PhD in population genetics, and while I’d picked up considerable experience with endangered-species reproduction at the theoretical level, my hands-on experience was limited. But nothing helps you adapt quickly to a new job like being asked to help the world’s most famous bear survive as a species.

When I arrived at the National Zoo, it had been almost three years since the birth of Mei Xiang and Tian Tian’s first cub, Tai Shan. There are usually two years between births, so the mood at the zoo was not worried, but determined. I was added to the weekly panda meeting, which included the curator, keepers, veterinarians, scientists, and even the zoo’s director—at the time I started it was John Berry, but segued to Dennis Kelly in 2010—as we pored over every detail of the breeding and birthing plans, making micro adjustments, to ensure not just a successful fertilization, but that Mei Xiang had everything an expectant panda mother needed to be happy. It was as if we could group-project a healthy cub into existence. And although I understood the intense desire to see a panda cub, I quickly learned the interest focuses not just on the offspring, but includes the reproductive act itself. And that level of detailed attention was not just for the panda professionals, but for the hobbyists as well. I think of pandas as being more adorable than alluring, but for some reason, a great many people are obsessed with their sex lives.

In 1972, when President Nixon contacted The Washington Star to share the news that Ling-Ling and Hsing-Hsing would be coming to the capital, he told the reporter, “The problem, however, with pandas is that they don’t know how to mate.” Director Ted Reed was aware of the “sex problem” and determined to get ahead of it. As part of the preparations for the pandas’ arrival, even though the pair were still too young to breed, he hired the zoo’s first female scientist, Devra Kleiman, as a reproductive zoologist. Over the years, he wrote frequently to Smithsonian Secretary S.Dillon Ripley about the zoo’s efforts to breed the animals and wryly acknowledged his unusual fixation. Reed saw the yearslong failure not just as his own (especially after optimistically promising baby pandas for the bicentennial year in 1976) but even more generally as a “defeat for machismo; an utter humiliation for all zoo males.” That these two very dignified men were sending letters back and forth in which the director referred to himself as the Smithsonian’s “resident sex maniac,” and, later, how he regretted that “the great panda sex orgy of 1979” had not materialized, was not at all inappropriate.

Talking about sex is necessary for the people responsible for breeding endangered species, especially when you’re talking about the important work of making more pandas. When it comes to pandas, it seems that everyone’s inhibitions fall away. Other species don’t get that pass. At the zoo, we see parents’ natural modesty kick in as they describe an elephant’s erection as an extra tail or leg, or they rush their children past a pair of amorous orangutans that are “just playing.” But when it comes to pandas, even if the animals are in the midst of attempting the act, a wide swath of the public, including families, will line up to watch and cheer them on.

Maybe it’s because they’re so cute that they seem more pure than prurient. I think most of our preoccupation with the sex lives of giant pandas derives from the urban legend that, when it comes to the basics of reproduction, these bears are completely clueless. This is demonstrably false because modern pandas, Ailuropoda melanoleuca, and their precursors have been on the planet for millions of years. In the wild, the panda lifespan is roughly fifteen to twenty years, so if you do the math, you can see that it took a lot of successful panda procreation to carry the species into the twenty-first century. So, where did this idea originate? And why does it persist?

In the Company of Pandas: Exploring the Secrets of the World’s Favorite Bear by Dr. Brandie Smith is available wherever books are sold on November 10.

Dr. Brandie Smith is the John and Adrienne Mars Director of the Smithsonian’s National Zoo and Conservation Biology Institute. With decades of experience in animal care, conservation, and zoo management, she has overseen critical breeding programs for endangered species and has personally witnessed some of the most dramatic moments in panda conservation history at one of America’s premier zoos. Originally from Indiana, Pennsylvania, she grew up around animals and began her professional career as an intern at the Pittsburgh Zoo and the National Zoo.

Xbox overhauls its PC, web, and cloud apps with a faster design

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Xbox Insiders can start testing the new PC app today.

Xbox Insiders can start testing the new PC app today.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Microsoft is unveiling a new Xbox PC app that launches “up to 60 percent faster” than before, with an overhauled design to match new updates to Xbox Cloud Gaming on the web and smart TVs. Xbox Insiders will be able to start testing the updated Xbox PC app today, and the updated Xbox Cloud Gaming interface will start rolling out over the next few weeks.

Ever since Microsoft launched its Xbox Ally handheld devices last year, it was clear the Xbox PC app still needed a lot of work to feel more like an Xbox console experience on Windows. Microsoft has greatly simplified the app now, with a design that no longer feels like you’re clicking a mouse when you’re using a controller.

“The app now launches up to 60 percent faster,” says Jason Beaumont, VP of Xbox experiences. “Pages like Home, Store, and Game Pass load up to 50 percent faster, and in-app windows open up to 65 percent faster.” The Xbox PC app also has simplified navigation that should make it easier to use on a controller, and the library section now has updated filters and better ways to organize your installed games.

Microsoft has improved the social parts of the Xbox PC app, too, with friends now listed in the main page and quick buttons to start a chat or join a party. The app overall feels less like a web one now, with what appears to be a mix of native and web elements. Microsoft has also refreshed the game card UI, as well as the home and settings sections. Even the animations have been updated to make this feel more like an Xbox dashboard experience.

I’ve been using this new Xbox PC app for the past few weeks, and it’s noticeably faster on a variety of devices I own. I’ve found it’s much easier to navigate around with a controller, and the search interface no longer tries to pop up when you’re moving between sections.

This redesign does make the Game Bar interface on handhelds feel a little out of place now, though. I understand Microsoft is looking to improve that in the coming months, and there should be design updates to make it match more closely to this updated Xbox PC app.

This new UI is also coming to Xbox Cloud Gaming. “We’re also bringing the refreshed Xbox web experience to more players and rolling out updates to Xbox Cloud Gaming apps on supported TVs and VR headsets,” says Beaumont. “These changes are part of our work to make Xbox faster, simpler, and more familiar wherever you play.”

The refreshed web version of Xbox Cloud Gaming has a similar look and feel to the Xbox PC app. A new library tab highlights cloud games across your own library and Xbox Game Pass. A desktop mode makes better use of larger displays, and you can choose between dark and light modes, as well as five new high-contrast options designed to improve accessibility support.

A similar design will also be available on smart TVs and VR headsets soon, but these designs aren’t exactly the same across PC, web, and smart TVs as Microsoft has customized them. “Playing on a PC, TV, or VR headset is different, and each experience needs to work well for the screen you’re using,” says Beaumont. “Wherever you play, the basics should feel familiar: finding your games, knowing what you can play, and getting into a game quickly.”

Santo António Primary School Requalification and Modernization Project / BFJ Arquitectos

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Text description provided by the architects. Built in the early 1950s under the Plano dos Centenários and designed by architect Inácio Peres Fernandes in 1947, the school is part of the urban model of the Alvalade neighbourhood, based on the concept of the neighbourhood unit. The complex retains most of its original features and is recognised as representative heritage of the first phase of this plan. Originally designed with separate male and female sections, some aspects no longer suited its current mixed-use operation. One example was the duplication of cafeteria spaces on two floors, which made kitchen operations inefficient.

Text description provided by the architects. Nintendo Is Currently Not Considering More Switch 2 Price Changes

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Nintendo president, Shuntaro Furukawa, has confirmed that the company is currently not considering any further price changes for the Switch 2 following an increase earlier this year.

This comes via Reuters after Nintendo released its latest financial data, during which it was reported that sales for the Switch 2 dropped over 37% year-on-year, with operating profit sliding 16%.

Despite some concern amongst investors, Furukawa-san has sought to ease this by confirming that Nintendo is currently in a position to secure sufficient quantities of memory as the supply crisis continues.

Nintendo's forecast for the remainder of the fiscal year remains unchanged, so it's currently expecting to shift over 16 million Switch 2 units; a decline from last year, sure, but still an incredibly impressive figure.

Confirmation that no further price changes are on the horizon will no doubt come as a relief for many. The Switch 2's price rose globally earlier this year as a result of market conditions, which has no doubt contributed to the downward turn in sales.

You could certainly argue, however, that the price hike was relatively tame compared to some of the changes taking place at other companies. The Switch 2's price rose by $50 in the US and a mere £24 in the UK. Any price hike is disappointing, of course, but when you consider how expensive the PS5 and Xbox Series X|S are these days, it could be a lot worse.

gustaf westman gives disney’s mickey & friends his signature chunky, colorful forms

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

the swedish designer translates familiar cartoon silhouettes into rounded household objects from colorful tableware to collectible furniture.

Swedish designer Gustaf Westman has teamed up with Disney to turn Mickey Mouse and his friends into a collection of sculptural furniture and household objects. Mickey’s oversized ears become a mirror, Donald Duck takes the shape of a colorful bookstand, and Goofy’s silhouette finds its way into furniture. Westman borrows details from the animated characters and reworks them through the rounded forms and exaggerated proportions that have come to define his Stockholm-based studio.

Gustaf Westman’s furniture has always had a cartoonish quality, with chunky silhouettes and bright colors that give ordinary household objects a more playful vibe. The designer works here with characters whose visual identities are already built around simple, recognizable shapes. The collection spans smaller pieces of tableware and decorative accessories, and extends all the way to sofas and larger furniture.

Among the smaller of Gustaf Westman’s objects for Disney is Mickey’s Trouser Vase, a bright red vessel shaped like the character’s shorts, complete with oversized white buttons. The Mickey Pipe Mirror takes an equally direct approach, tracing the outline of his round ears in polished metal tubing. Meanwhile, Donald Duck is interpreted as a sculptural bookstand, with his sailor hat, red bow tie and yellow feet arranged into a colorful composition of geometric forms.

Throughout the collection, recognizable character details take on entirely new functions. Donald’s sailor hat becomes a cup, while Goofy’s distinctive silhouette informs a sculptural vase. Mickey and Minnie also appear across a range of tableware, with their ears and accessories translated into the designer‘s usual bulbous forms.

The collaboration expands into larger furniture with the Mickey and Minnie sofas, whose exaggerated curves bring the characters into the living room. Westman also introduces a Goofy Coffee Table and Donald Shelf, building familiar animated silhouettes into functional objects. A Mickey Bar Cart continues the collection’s exploration of cartoon-inspired furniture, with forms that feel equally at home in Westman’s existing catalog.

Across the range, Westman uses surprisingly few details to suggest each character. A pair of rounded ears or a particular combination of colors is often enough. This approach allows the objects to retain their own sculptural identities, while drawing on the familiarity of Disney’s animated world. The characters become part of the furniture’s geometry, shaping the way each piece occupies space.

the Mickey tumbler borrows the red shorts, black ears and yellow shoes of the legendary mouseMinnie’s pink polka-dotted bow wraps around a matching cup and saucerMickey’s ears complete a glossy black ceramic cup and saucer

Los Angeles votes all-in on housing, electing YIMBY-backed Raman

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Nithya Raman defeated incumbent Karen Bass in Los Angeles as YIMBY groups framed the race around accelerating housing production. City timelines for statewide SB 79 transit oriented housing rules, currently delayed to 2030, are expected to face pressure under the new administration.  AI Summary

City Councilmember Nithya Raman defeated Mayor Karen Bass to become the 44th mayor of Los Angeles, a win for the “yes in my backyard” groups that backed her challenge to the incumbent.

The Associated Press called the race Thursday. Raman, an urban planner and member of the Democratic Socialists of America, led by 4.2 percentage points, 52.1% to 47.9%, with roughly 76% of the expected vote counted.

She is the second DSA member to lead a major U.S. city. New York Mayor Zohran Mamdani won a year ago on an affordability platform that made more housing a focal point. Bass conceded and promised a “smooth and seamless transition.” She is the first Los Angeles mayor in two decades to lose a reelection bid.

Raman won despite long odds. Gov. Gavin Newsom, former Vice President Kamala Harris, retiring former House Speaker Nancy Pelosi and nearly every City Council member backed Bass.

YIMBY Action, YIMBY Los Angeles and California YIMBY were among the housing groups behind Raman. Unions, progressive Democratic organizations, environmental groups and County Supervisor Lindsey Horvath also endorsed her.

Housing and homelessness drove the campaign. Raman pitched expanded medical teams for people in encampments and moving residents out of city-rented hotels into permanent housing. She said she could cut encampments by at least half by 2028.

YIMBY groups cast the race as a test of how fast the city builds. The movement pushes to ease zoning limits and speed approvals to expand supply.

“Her win in California’s largest city is a big moment for the pro-housing movement,” California YIMBY posted on social media.

One early test is how Los Angeles complies with Senate Bill 79, the state law that encourages more housing near transit. City ordinances that took effect June 30 hold off full compliance citywide until 2030. YIMBY advocates will likely press the new mayor and council to move that date up. Raman favored a quicker housing timeline.

Mayor-elect Raman could inherit a $20 million-plus liability if the city doesn’t approve Woodland Hills Country Club’s 398-home plan. The club’s attorney, Dave Rand, a Raman supporter, set a 60-day deadline, and the state housing department wants a response by Nov. 25.

Raman will need the council to do that. Most members backed Bass, so Raman must build a majority from a body that largely opposed her.

She will have allies elsewhere at City Hall. Controller Kenneth Mejia and newly elected City Attorney Marissa Roy, a DSA member, join her in what The Hollywood Reporter called a “triumvirate of progressivism” at the top of city government.

Canary’s Injective ETF debuts on Nasdaq with exposure to staking rewards

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

An Injective affiliate recently became an SEC-registered transfer agent amid an expanding tokenization push.

Canary Capital has launched the Canary Staked INJ ETF, offering investors exposure to Injective's native INJ token. The fund will trade on the Nasdaq under the ticker INJC.

The ETF seeks to earn additional INJ through in Injective's proof-of-stake validation process, with net staking rewards reflected in its net asset value.

"Injective was designed from the ground up for trading, tokenization, markets, and financial applications," said Canary Capital CEO Steven McClurg in a press release. "We believe investors are increasingly looking past the largest digital assets and toward the specialized networks building the next generation of on-chain financial infrastructure."

Injective is a Layer 1 blockchain focused on onchain financial applications, including trading, derivatives, and tokenization. Its native token was up more than 5% on Thursday.

In August, Injective's affiliate, Injective Institutional Services, became an SEC-registered transfer agent amid an expanding tokenization push.

"In terms of U.S. licensing, I think transfer agent is basically the very start," Injective CEO Eric Chen told The Block last month, noting that "an Injective institution can actually be the clearinghouse for settlement."

Injective says it has tokenized over $1 billion in assets to date. As of Oct. 22, Injective had processed more than 3.13 billion transactions and permanently burned more than 7.27 million INJ across its monthly token buybacks.

"Investors can now gain exposure to INJ through the brokerage accounts they already use," Chen said Thursday in a release. "We built Injective to bring financial markets onchain. Canary's launch connects that mission to a broader base of investors, giving them a familiar way to access the asset that powers Injective."

There are two other proposed ETF applications tied to INJ: the 21Shares Injective ETF and the REX-Osprey INJ Staking ETF.

Agility Robotics Starts Nasdaq Trading After Churchill SPAC Merger

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Agility Robotics announced on November 5, 2026 that its common stock and warrants had begun trading on Nasdaq after completing its business combination with Churchill Capital Corp XI, with the transaction and related financings providing approximately $465 million in gross capital before transaction expenses.

The company describes the listing as making it the first U.S. publicly listed pure-play humanoid robotics company with proven, active commercial deployments, citing nearly three years of real-world deployment experience and more than 65,000 hours of operation. Digit is operating with GXO, Schaeffler and Toyota Motor Manufacturing Canada.

The debut followed Agility’s November 4, 2026 completion announcement, which said the Churchill transaction had closed and that Nasdaq trading was expected to begin on November 5, 2026.

The gross capital includes proceeds from a private investment in public equity led by Foxconn and $100 million of equity funding received before closing. Agility intends to use the proceeds to fulfill customer orders, expand commercial deployments, scale Digit production and continue advancing its integrated robotics and physical AI platform.

CEO Peggy Johnson said becoming a public company gives Agility access to more resources to accelerate its progress and bring intelligent robots to more workplaces.

Digit 5 has more than $300 million in committed multi-year orders, according to Agility, which expects early customer access in the first half of 2027 and targets general availability for the end of that year. The commitments are subject to contractual milestones, product features and specifications, making the total conditional order volume rather than realized revenue. The robot was unveiled in September 2026.

Digit has moved more than 100,000 totes at the existing GXO deployment in Georgia, Agility reports, and an expanded agreement is expected to bring the robot to two additional U.S. logistics facilities in early 2027. AGCO has signed a commercial agreement for a Digit pilot expected in 2027.

Three additional enterprise manufacturing customers are participating in paid programs for specific manufacturing applications. Agility reports a pipeline of more than 30 prospective customers across automotive, manufacturing, aerospace, logistics, consumer products and defense.

Digit 5’s proprietary cycloidal actuators are engineered for repetitive lifting of up to 50 pounds, according to Agility, and the platform is designed for cooperatively safe work closer to people. Interchangeable end effectors and expanded manipulation capabilities are intended to support a broader range of industrial workflows. The system is supported by Agility’s proprietary robotics and physical AI technology, safety systems and Agility Arc, its cloud-based software for managing robot fleets and integrating them into customer operations.

RoboFab, a 70,000-square-foot manufacturing facility in Salem, Oregon, is designed for annual production capacity of up to 10,000 robots, according to Agility. That capacity is a design specification rather than current output. The company’s Fremont, California, facility focuses on hardware and physical AI development, while an announced 50,000-square-foot Pittsburgh expansion is intended to strengthen software, AI, deployment and customer-support capabilities.

The public listing pairs fresh capital and substantial designed factory capacity with an order book that still depends on product and contractual milestones. Turning those commitments into deployed robots at the scale the financing and RoboFab are intended to support is the central execution test between Agility’s current operating record and its planned commercial expansion.

2027 Garden Planning: What the Experts Say Will Be Big Next Year

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 5, 2026 4:51 PM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

Tips, tricks & ideas for a better home and yard, delivered to your inbox daily.

There’s always something new and exciting on the horizon in the world of gardening. Around this time every year, various organizations release their favorite gardening and landscaping trends for the coming year. From the color of the year to new plants and outdoor décor, the list below represents our favorite 2027 gardening trends for you to consider as you plan or update your outdoor space in the coming months.

Garden Media Group has identified ancient blue as the garden color of the year for 2027. As the first manufactured color in history, blue was created by the Egyptians 5,000 years ago to imitate precious lapis lazuli. While blue is consistently the favorite color around the world, it is the rarest color in the garden, showing up in fewer than 10 percent of flowers. “This makes growing it in the garden feel like victory,” says Katie Dubow, president of Garden Media Group.

Here are some planting and décor tips to seamlessly incorporate this calming color into your garden:

Gardeners prefer more from their edible plants than just fruits and vegetables to harvest, according to Monrovia’s 2027 Garden Design Trends Study. “We’re seeing shoppers be more discerning in their edible choices,” says Katie Tamony, chief marketing officer and trend spotter at Monrovia. “They’re looking for plants that add a design element or feel more exotic than the common choices their neighbors grow. People are also interested in edible plants that look good all season long.”

Adding an edible garden ranks among the highest-interest projects in Monrovia’s survey, with more than 45 percent of respondents saying they are looking for more unique selections. Enhance your edible garden with specialty herbs; berries with year-round interest; and conversation-starting selections like pomegranate, figs, kiwi, and olives.

After years of beige minimalism and tidy rows of shrubs, the “Let’s Go Rococo” theme is all about exuberance over restraint, says Dubow. Emerging in early 18th century France, rococo design was a deliberate rebellion by artists and craftsmen against the stiff grandeur of Baroque and societal wealth inequality. Think flowers, shells, gilded flourishes, ruffles, jewel-tone palettes, and asymmetry. “It’s ornamental, romantic, a little over the top, and joyful,” notes Dubow.

Here are some ways to infuse rococo style into your garden:

Garden Media Group’s OutdooRx trend positions the garden as medicine, says Dubow. “In the United Kingdom, doctors now “socially prescribe” gardening programs to patients, and research continues to show that gardening lowers stress hormones like cortisol while boosting mood-lifting dopamine and serotonin,” she explains. Approaches like the Sunday Garden Trend can provide relaxation.

Fortunately, you don’t need a prescription to benefit. You can create a therapeutic garden in your own backyard with these helpful tips:

This long-handle digging tool allows gardeners to work the soil more comfortably (and for longer periods of time) from a standing position.

National Garden Bureau also tracks garden trends, especially when it comes to which specific plants are the most popular in a given year. “Coming up in 2027, we see an enormous interest in hellebores, and that is our Year of the Crop for Perennials,” says Diane Blazek, executive director for All-America Selections at National Garden Bureau.

Often called the Christmas rose or Lenten rose, the hellebore is a hardy perennial that does well in full shade and brings beauty to winter and early spring gardens when most other flowers have faded. It produces elegant 2- to 3-inch blossoms in a cup shape that resemble roses. Hellebore flowers grow in a variety of colors, so you have plenty of options. It grows in USDA Hardiness Zones 5 to 8, and does best in cottage gardens, winter gardens, or woodland gardens. Take note that this plant is toxic and should be handled with caution.

As another season of the popular show The Gilded Age is released this month, romance and beauty are alive and well in gardens, too. This look maximizes the popularity of European-inspired garden design with an elevated, modern twist, shining a spotlight on classic plants like roses, peonies, hydrangeas, and boxwood. In addition to these timeless plants, this trend showcases artful hedges, chartreuse foliage, and collector statement plants.

“This trend appeals to established gardeners,” says Tamony. “Homeowners who have invested in their interior design are taking that high-end look to the garden.” Their interest in rare, collector plants is increasing, and Monrovia’s research shows their willingness to pay for premium plants and exquisite blooms is up between 5 and 9 percent since 2025.

Another style that is expected to be popular is the Palm Springs outdoor space. This works well for gardeners looking to create an attractive desert oasis through containers as well as the landscape. “The Palm Springs Style trend is all about creating a calming, wellness-focused outdoor space that feels more upscale,” says Tamony. With this aesthetic, gardeners can transform their landscape by choosing structured succulents and lush, tropical colors found with species from Agave, Bougainvillea, Mandevilla, and tropical hibiscus.

We’ll help you set up your first garden—whether that’s a few pots on your patio, a raised bed, or an in-ground plot out back—and select the right plants for your soil and region.

How Big of a Calorie Deficit Do You Need to Achieve Body Recomp?

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Your calorie deficit could be working against you. Here’s how to find the right balance.

With body recomposition come two goals: lose body fat while gaining muscle. At first glance, it might seem a bit too good to be true, if you think about how each goal is achieved individually. But it’s not.

To lose body fat, you need to eat fewer calories than you burn in a day. To gain muscle, you need to continuously challenge your muscles in the gym, and eat enough to hit your protein goals, which is vital to building muscle mass—especially in a calorie deficit.

With the two goals seemingly at nutritional odds, it’s difficult to determine exactly how you should eat to hit this two-for-one goal. Exactly how big of a calorie deficit do you need to achieve body recomp? We tapped an expert to find out.

Get your personalized plan with our Eating for Body Recomp guide

Achieving body recomp takes some strategic nutritional planning, starting with a balanced calorie goal.

Keep your calorie deficit on the more conservative side when it comes to body recomp so you have enough energy to power your important workouts, Dana White, RD, previously told WH. Aim for a calorie deficit no larger than 250 to 500 calories per day. Any larger than that, and you run the risk of feeling under-fueled in the gym or not hitting your protein goals—which is major for building and maintaining muscle mass.

With body recomp, though, it’s important to get even more granular and understand what’s making up those cals, says White. “It’s not just about eating calories—it’s the type of calories.”

One of the best ways to determine how to fill those calories is by tracking your macronutrients—carbohydrates, proteins, and fats. To find out how much of each macro you should get, check out our Eating for Body Recomp guide.

Cori Ritchey, C.S.C.S., is the fitness editor at Women's Health, as well as a certified strength and condition coach and group fitness instructor. She’s reported on topics regarding health, nutrition, mental health, fitness, sex, and relationships for several years. You can find more of her work in Men’s Health, HealthCentral, Livestrong, Self, and others.

Hyundai Recalls 38K Vehicles Over Faulty Airbags and Seatbelts

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The recall covers certain Veloster N and Kona Electric models and stems from a software error.

Hyundai issued a recall over a concerning safety issue that could affect as many as 37,901 vehicles, documents filed with the National Highway Traffic Safety Administration (NHTSA) show. According to the recall paperwork, the recall covers some 2019–2022 Veloster N and 2019–2023 Kona Electric vehicles, whose airbags and seatbelt pretensioners may not deploy in a crash.

The recall population is split between 8843 Velosters and 29,058 Konas. According to the recall documents, the issue with the airbags and seatbelt pretensioners originates from a software error. Each of the affected cars comes equipped with an Airbag Control Unit, or ACU, that detects crash signals and instructs the airbags and seatbelt pretensioners to deploy. The NHTSA documents show that the ACU’s software may be corrupted when the system reboots while the ACU is writing data to its internal memory. At a minimum, there is a warning to notify drivers of the issue. If the ACU’s data is corrupted, the airbag malfunction indicator light will illuminate on the dashboard.

See all results for used 2022 Hyundai Veloster N for sale near 19355

The recall comes after more than a year of testing and attempts to recreate the issue by both Hyundai and the ACU’s supplier, following an increase in airbag warranty light claims that was identified in August 2024. As of October 2026, Hyundai was aware of 331 reported incidents alleging the recall complaint, though, thankfully, the company was not aware of any crashes, fires, or injuries.

Owners of affected vehicles will be instructed to bring their car to a Hyundai dealer, where technicians will update the ACU software. The update will be performed for free, and Hyundai will reimburse owners for out-of-pocket expenses incurred to get the fix before the recall was filed. Letters notifying both Hyundai dealers and affected owners of the recall will be mailed on January 1, 2027.

Jack Fitzgerald’s love for cars stems from his as yet unshakable addiction to Formula 1. After a brief stint as a detailer for a local dealership group in college, he knew he needed a more permanent way to drive all the new cars he couldn’t afford and decided to pursue a career in auto writing. By hounding his college professors at the University of Wisconsin-Milwaukee, he was able to travel Wisconsin seeking out stories in the auto world before landing his dream job at Car and Driver. His new goal is to delay the inevitable demise of his 2010 Volkswagen Golf.

XRP Price Prediction: Wall Street’s $28 Target Hinges on Supply Shock

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Ahmed Barakat is a journalist and copywriter based in Georgia with a growing focus on blockchain technology, DeFi, AI, privacy, digital assets, and fintech innovation.

The CryptoNews editorial team is composed of seasoned writers specializing in cryptocurrency and blockchain technology. Their expertise ensures comprehensive, accurate, and insightful content for...

XRP is trading near $1.30 as institutional demand and tightening supply return to the spotlight. Standard Chartered still sees XRP price reaching $28 despite cutting its near-term prediction.

The bank’s target would require XRP to climb more than 2,000% from current levels. That makes institutional adoption and sustained buying pressure central to the bullish case.

U.S. spot XRP ETFs have accumulated 1.2 billion XRP since launching. Meanwhile, Ripple’s escrow system continues releasing tokens, creating a potential clash between institutional demand and available supply.

Standard Chartered’s Geoffrey Kendrick lowered his 2026 XRP price target to $2.80 earlier this year. However, the bank maintained its long-term forecast of $28 by 2030. Its revised roadmap projects XRP reaching $7 in 2027, $12.60 in 2028, and $19.60 in 2029. The final $28 target would represent an extraordinary recovery from current prices.

At approximately $1.30, XRP would need to gain around 2,050% to reach $28. Its market capitalization would approach $1.8 trillion using today’s circulating supply.

This valuation would place XRP among the world’s largest financial assets. ETF demand alone cannot justify such a move without substantial growth in adoption and liquidity.

Kendrick’s bullish thesis relies on institutional participation, regulatory clarity, and greater use of blockchain payment infrastructure. Spot ETFs provide one potential route for traditional investors to gain exposure.

The question now is whether those products can absorb enough XRP to influence market supply.

Discover: The Crypto Presales This Q4 2026

U.S. spot XRP ETFs held 1.2 billion XRP by November 4. That accounts for 1.2% of the token’s fixed 100 billion maximum supply.

The funds have accumulated substantial holdings since their launch. However, recent flows show that institutional demand has not been consistently positive.

ETF data recorded outflows during several recent sessions, while October’s strongest buying days were followed by withdrawals. This could create a supply squeeze with renewed and sustained inflows.

If ETF demand accelerates, issuers must acquire XRP to support their products. Those purchases could reduce the amount of XRP readily available for trading, particularly if existing holders refuse to sell.

That dynamic could amplify price movements during periods of rising demand. A genuine supply shock would require persistent buying to overwhelm available sellers across exchanges and other markets.

Ripple’s escrow system adds another layer to the XRP price prediction. The company releases up to one billion XRP from escrow each month, with unused tokens often returned to new escrow contracts. Ripple is reportedly holding 31.7 billion XRP in escrow. Its total XRP holdings stood at 36.8 billion.

The negative impact of the 31 October release by Ripple of 300 million XRP from escrow🤣. $1.23 to $1.31. It’s as if XRP moves with the market or something. pic.twitter.com/OX3md8GTP1

But could XRP touch $28? The bullish scenario would require ETF purchases and institutional accumulation to outpace selling pressure. Greater use of XRP in payments and tokenized financial markets could provide additional demand.

XRP price remains far below Standard Chartered’s long-term prediction, for now. The token’s ability to hold recent support and attract renewed ETF inflows will help determine the supply-squeeze thesis and whether it can gain traction.

A sustained institutional buying cycle could strengthen the case for a much larger rally. Reaching $28, however, would require years of adoption growth beyond the ETF market alone.

Chinese AI labs are attempting to export their data since they cannot import Nvidia GPUs.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Moonshot AI, the Chinese lab that made Kimi, is reportedly looking towards Europe to gain access to the latest Nvidia Blackwell AI GPUs. According to CNBC, the company is in talks with at least one European data center to lease clusters with 8,000 or more GPUs for inferencing, and that it’s also interested in securing compute capacity for training.

While Chinese companies are banned from acquiring Nvidia Blackwell and other AI GPUs that offer the same or better performance containing U.S. technologies, a loophole has so far allowed them to gain access to compute by renting them from data centers abroad. The U.S. House is attempting to patch it and passed the Remote Access Security Act, but it must still go through the Senate and the White House before it can turn into law. In the meantime, Chinese AI labs are exploiting the loophole and training their models on foreign data centers that have acquired Nvidia chips.

The U.S. and China have been locked in an AI arms race, with the latter’s open-weight models behind frontier U.S. models by just a matter of months. More importantly, these models, like Kimi K3, are significantly easier to run and could even be much cheaper to use. These advancements happened despite Washington’s efforts to make it harder for Chinese AI labs to acquire hardware that uses American technologies through export bans. But even though President Donald Trump eventually made a 180-degree turn and allowed Nvidia H200 chips for export to China, Beijing has taken steps to protect its domestic AI chip industry by blocking H200 imports at the border, meaning these companies must look elsewhere if they want to access the latest, most powerful AI GPUs to train their models.

Moonshot also has a limited window if it wants to secure compute capacity outside of China — Nvidia has reportedly told its data center clients to limit their business and avoid serving more than 49% of their capacity to Chinese outfits. This could be a harbinger of things to come, especially as reports like these could spotlight the bipartisan bill and increase pressure to advance the legislation sooner rather than later.

Bitcoin Price Trades Near $76,000 as Nasdaq Tumbles into Correction

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Bitcoin price traded near $76,000 on Thursday afternoon, down more than 5% on the day after sliding through the mid-$75,000s as a Nasdaq-led selloff in chip and AI stocks deepened into a formal correction and forced out more than $1 billion in leveraged bets, Bitcoin Magazine Pro data shows.

The afternoon session extended the morning crash that had already erased overnight gains above $80,000. Bitcoin briefly tested support near $75,400 before stabilizing just under $76,000 into the final hour of U.S. cash trading.

Bitcoin near $75,900 in afternoon trade, on track for its biggest single-day slide since June. The move undid Wednesday’s short-squeeze bounce in a single session. Traders who bought the midterm rebound with borrowed money were the first forced out when stocks cracked at the open, and the bitcoin price never regained the $77,000 area that held as morning support.

Wall Street set the tone. The Nasdaq Composite fell about 5% on Thursday and dropped into correction territory, more than 10% below the record close it set last month, CNBC reported. The S&P 500 lost roughly 4%, and the Dow shed more than 1,600 points.

Semiconductors led the rout. The Philadelphia Semiconductor Index sank about 11%, and the iShares Semiconductor ETF posted its biggest one-day drop since March 2020. Nvidia and Broadcom each fell around 9%, Qualcomm lost about 9% and Tesla dropped about 11%.

Investors are pricing in a slower AI data-center buildout under a Democratic Congress. Traders fear construction pauses and tighter financing for neoclouds, a risk that has weighed on chip and infrastructure names since the midterm results. Analysts told CNBC that the $1.2 trillion of AI capital spending forecast for next year is now being reworked.

Bitcoin has moved with that AI-heavy complex for most of the year. Miners that sold bitcoin to fund data centers are now caught in both trades, so political risk on power and construction hits the same names that used to trade mainly on the bitcoin price.

Forced selling kept the bitcoin price under pressure into the afternoon. Roughly $1.08 billion in leveraged crypto positions were liquidated over 24 hours, including about $954 million in long bets, as futures markets unwound positions built during Wednesday’s short squeeze.

Nearly $900 million of that wipeout came in a roughly four-hour stretch after the U.S. open, turning a stock-led break into a cascade of margin calls. Ether fell below $2,300 as the same leverage flushed out.

Crypto equities followed. Strategy (MSTR), Coinbase (COIN) and Circle (CRCL) each fell more than 10%, and bitcoin miners dropped as much as 20%. Keel Infrastructure (KEEL), which sold its entire bitcoin treasury for $192 million, slid about 20%. CleanSpark (CLSK), Cipher (CIFR) and TeraWulf (WULF) each lost more than 12%.

The next test is Friday’s October jobs report, then October CPI on Nov. 10 and the Fed’s Dec. 9 decision. Policy risk is building too, with House Democrats drafting their own market structure bill ahead of taking the committees in January.

Traders are watching whether the bitcoin price can hold the mid-$75,000 area that served as support on election night and several times since August. A clean break would leave bitcoin with room to run lower, near its weakest levels in months and put the September low back in view.

Options markets have been quiet into the jobs print, but the cash market is not. Volume stayed elevated through the afternoon as stocks and crypto sold together rather than against each other.

At the time of writing, the bitcoin price is near $75,900.

At least 27 flights across Europe were disrupted Thursday by bomb threats, the second wave in a week, and no explosives were found in any case. French fighter jets intercepted an Aer Lingus Dublin–Rome flight carrying more than 150 passengers and escorted it to an air base in Normandy, Bulgaria's air force escorted three airliners to the ground, and Madrid authorities searched Air Europa and Iberia aircraft after receiving three emailed threats in under 20 minutes. Earlier threats on Monday and Tuesday hit flights operated by Wizz Air, Ryanair, LOT, and Scandinavian Airlines, plus a cruise ship in Greece. No government has said who is responsible, and Bloomberg reported no pattern in the carriers or routes targeted. Matthew Borie of Osprey Flight Solutions told the AP the threats could be copycats, a single person or group, or "hybrid warfare" by state actors, and pointed to a possible Russian link; Moscow has denied European accusations of sabotage this year. The operational math is the problem: each threat an airline acts on means a diversion or stopped departure, a full search, often a replacement aircraft, crews timing out, and passengers rebooked — and Skift has reported that insurance typically doesn't cover those commercial losses. The hoaxes arrive while European carriers are already absorbing jet fuel prices that have more than doubled year over year, Middle East airspace closures that lengthen Asia routes, and drone incursions at airports.

Serve Robotics Opens Fleet Software and Data to Physical AI Companies

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Serve Robotics announced Physical AI Services on November 5, 2026, making its robot-connectivity, data-curation and real-world training-data tools available to other companies building physical AI. Bedrock Robotics, which develops autonomous systems for heavy construction equipment, is among the launch customers.

The three-product suite productizes infrastructure Serve built while expanding its own operations. The company says it has deployed more than 2,000 robots in more than 40 cities and 27 hospitals, and that Physical AI Services already powers thousands of vehicles across a dozen industries. Those include construction, mining, autonomous trucking and Serve’s sidewalk food-delivery fleet, giving the offering an operating base that spans different machines and environments.

“Building a robot is only the beginning. Keeping thousands of them connected, learning and improving requires another layer of technology,” said co-founder and CEO Ali Kashani.

Serve Link connects machines in the field to models, monitoring and training systems as more physical AI computation moves into data centers. Its functions include task management, unit-status monitoring, remote assistance for exception handling, data collection and hard-example mining. Bedrock uses Link to connect equipment over Wi-Fi, LTE, 5G and satellite networks.

The Physical AI Services documentation says the software can bond 4G, 5G, Wi-Fi, satellite and private cellular connections, predicts optimal transmission logic to avoid buffering, and is designed to reduce latency, processing time and compute overhead while running on almost any hardware. Those capabilities are intended to keep fleets reachable across varying locations and network conditions.

Serve Data Curator addresses the volume of information produced by deployed robots. It converts petabytes of sensor input into searchable, visualizable fleet data, allowing engineers to find relevant scenarios, investigate failures and identify long-tail edge cases without manually reviewing every recording.

Selected examples can be assembled into focused datasets for training and evaluation. Integrated annotation and connections to machine-learning workflows link the discovery of useful examples to their use in model development. Model performance can then guide which examples teams curate, collect and simulate next, connecting what robots encounter in the field with subsequent training.

Serve says Data Catalog contains millions of hours of real-world data from robots operating on streets, including topographical and infrastructure information, and that live-deployment collection respects local laws and the privacy of people nearby. Terrain and infrastructure data from sidewalk deployments are intended to teach foundation models about complex human environments such as city sidewalks.

The suite is modular and plug-and-play, with documented uses in humanoid robotics, agriculture, forestry, underwater exploration and autonomous trucks alongside delivery and construction. Its common thread is infrastructure: connectivity, data transport and curation are offered as existing foundations so customers can devote engineering time to their own machines and autonomy.

For Serve, the central question is how far that operating experience can transfer. The offering asks customers to build on infrastructure and data shaped by sidewalk and hospital deployments while their own machines tackle different tasks; its value rests on how much engineering work those shared foundations can remove.
