Former Man Utd chairman slams owners after controversial Avram Glazer comments

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Former Manchester United chairman Martin Edwards expects supporters to be furious with Avram Glazer’s comments on the club and has accused the owners of getting their priorities wrong.

The Athletic report that Glazer made a controversial point while speaking to financiers at Deutsche Bank’s inaugural Sports Investment Conference in London in September.

One of the owners of the Red Devils compared Manchester United to the New York Yankees and Dallas Cowboys as a brand that will remain commercially successful irrespective of how well they are performing on the pitch.

Edwards, who was chairman of Manchester United from 1980-2003, expects fans to react badly to that sentiment and has encouraged the current owners to train all their focus on improving the team.

Sign up to our daily football newsletter, pick your team and get the latest news sent directly to your inbox every morning.

‘I heard that this morning and I thought “By Jove, what’s the reaction going to be from the supporters?” Supporters don’t want to hear that!’ Edwards said on talkSPORT.

‘Supporters want to hear that you’re winning trophies. They’re not interested in profits or dividends or what people are taking out of the club. They want the club to be successful, they want the best players, they’d rather be spending money on the team than on the new stadium.’

Edwards accepts that running a club the size of Manchester United is a tricky business and owners must satisfy various stakeholders, but he feels the balance is currently off.

‘When I was running United, I always felt I had three responsibilities, really,’ he said. ‘One: in those days we were still developing the ground, so I had to build the ground and develop the ground. Look after the team, which is always the most important. But also, because we were public we had to pay dividends and keep the shareholders happy.

‘It’s a balance. Running a football club is a complete balance, from top to balance. It’s a question of whether you get it right or wrong.’

Use AI to go deeper into the stories you care about – powered by Metro and trusted publications.

He added: ‘I think it’s quite clear, in my eyes, that the team is the most important element at the moment. You’ve got to get the team right and forget new stadiums if you’re not going to fill them. Get the team winning again and get the supporters behind you. We’re not a united front at the moment.’

The landscape of top flight football has changed considerably since Edwards was in charge at Manchester United and he believes it has not all been for the better.

‘You’ve got owners now who don’t attend games. They put somebody in charge, whatever else,’ he said. ‘Existing owners now, they wouldn’t know who’s in the team.

‘It’s become big business and what you can get out of it. The game has changed and for the older ones of us it’s disappointing, really, the direction it’s gone.’

Looking back to the start of Manchester United’s decline as a title-challenging force in the Premier League, Edwards suggests it was the loss of Alex Ferguson and CEO David Gill in 2013 and Ed Woodward being named as Gill’s replacement.

‘Once Alec and David Gill decided to leave in 2013, that was a huge blow to the club and they’ve never really recovered from that,’ he said.

‘If you wanted to be critical you’d say right from the beginning when they replaced David Gill, they replaced him with not a football man, but a merchant banker.

‘If you’ve got the top of the football chain who’s not converse with football, that does make it difficult. I think that once the mistakes start, they multiply.

‘Recruitment has been a big problem. Look at the players they’ve bought, some of them haven’t been up to the standard. Generally it snowballs. That’s been the problem.

‘Also, have United spent enough money to be able to compete at the top? Manchester City have had a huge advantage in the short-term because they haven’t been sticking to the rules and other people have. That’s given them a huge advantage, so we probably couldn’t have competed with them, but we could certainly have competed with Liverpool and Arsenal and whatever else.’

These Are The Recessionmobiles You Want Automakers To Build

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Folks, it sure feels like we're trapped in the belly of a terrible machine and the machine is bleeding to death. The AI bubble threatens to pop and take the global economy with it, war with Iran is unending, we've heard it all before — the old world dying, the new one struggling to be born, yadda yadda. But even in the worst of times, we've all got to go to work and buy our groceries and take the kids to school, so we need cars that can ferry us through the time of monsters.

That's why, earlier this week, I asked you all what recessionmobiles automakers should build to get us through the next once-in-a-lifetime global financial crisis. Your answers took inspiration from all sorts of sections of the automotive world, from the oil embargo of the '70s to the very modern segment of electrically aided micro mobility. Let's dive in and see what you suggested.

Midsize sedans that start in the low $20s and end around $30 fully loaded. In a lot of trim levels, with manual transmissions and optional "big" engines that can be paired with those manuals as well. Wagon versions, coupe versions, AWD for those who want to pay an extra $1k. I miss the golden era of the family sedan from '00-'10.

We're already seeing it; e-bikes and e-scooters. Once we're all unemployed, and fuel goes to $8/gallon, it will be the only way we can afford to get around.

A recession coinciding with high fuel prices, or a possible physical shortage of fuel? The smallest and most efficient vehicles possible.

It's say just take the physically smaller half of every manufacturer's current lineup and add an actual base model trim that does away with the infotainment system, every ADAS feature, and leather/fake-leather seats, etc. Probably would've trim off much from the MSRP, but at least then there might be some new cars I'd actually consider buying.

"Automakers should sell me a Slate, but for more money" is certainly a unique take.

Chevy: Waving their hands around pointing at the Bolt which starts at 28k before incentives and seats four comfortably, with hatchback versatility and lifestyle-suggestive crossover styling, impervious to oil price swings caused by various international conflicts

I think that the rest of the world should be building cheap EVs, saving a ton of money on fuel, and being extremely demonstrative about it.

Every domestic manufacturer should offer basic subcompact 2-door and compact 4-door cars with a non-turbo 3 or 4 cylinder manual transmissions and a compact regular-cab 3 or 4-cylinder pickup truck and price them at $15,000 or below.

Imagine the possibilities if there was that much competition in the compact car and truck market. People say small cars and trucks don't sell in America, but they will if they're priced correctly.

2 words, Light and Cheap. Make something that doesn't weigh 2 tons to start. Bring back 4 cylinder non turbo cars that don't need 300+hp just to get up to highway speed. As others have said, the Maverick and Slate have shown there's a market for basic transportation. We need a modern Model T, not another Expedition.

Budget sub-brands. GM had Saturn, and Toyota had Scion. Toyota could take advantage of economies of scale to resurrect Scion as a budget-friendly, hybrid only brand. Use the Prius and Camry platforms and put quirky, boxy bodies on them for maximum utility.

Ex Chelsea manager Liam Rosenior says sacking had 'nothing to do with football'

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Chelsea fired Liam Rosenior earlier this year, with the manager now explaining what went wrong at Stamford Bridge

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Former Chelsea manager Liam Rosenior has opened up on his time managing the Blues, and his sacking after just 23 games in charge.

Rosenior was drafted in at Chelsea from their sister club, Strasbourg. to replace the outgoing Enzo Maresca in January 2026 on a six-and-a-half-year deal, winning his first match in charge and overseeing a positive start to his tenure.

But the West Londoners pulled the trigger in April, with the club seventh in the table – and Rosenior has since taken up the manager's job at Paris FC.

In an interview with So Foot, relayed by Sport Witness, Rosenior has opened up about his time in charge of the Blues, and the “snowball effect” in the media surrounding results at Stamford Bridge.

“It was the best experience of my life, and the hardest too,” the 42-year-old said. “It was fantastic, even though it only lasted four months.”

“Actually, the moment that worried me the most was at the beginning, when we went through that five-game winning streak.”

“Any club would have been happy with such a run, but not Chelsea. You get good results and you’re in the spotlight. Then, there was a kind of snowball effect in the media.”

The best features, fun and footballing quizzes, straight to your inbox every week.

Rosenior found himself mocked for his approach: he became a figure of ridicule for handing substitute Alejandro Garnacho a note while losing 8-2 on aggregate, and for David Brent-esque quotes such as “respecting the ball” and “ageing men”.

“They made fun of those instructions I gave Garnacho on a scrap of paper when we were down to 10 men against PSG,” he continued. “The clothes I wore became a joke, the way I spoke too. I’m not a joke!

“You’re the first people I’m talking to about this, and probably the last for a long time, but one day, I’ll tell you what really happened.”

Since being relieved of duties, Rosenior has had another run-in with BlueCo, when he was subjected to abusive chants from fans of Strasbourg in September.

After Paris won the fixture, Rosenior appeared to celebrate in front of his former fans, perhaps signalling just how he truly feels towards his ex-employers.

Chelsea travel to Sunderland this weekend as Premier League action continues.

NYC pied-a-terre tax notices get an appeals court green light

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

A state appeals court dismissed a homeowners’ lawsuit that had disrupted NYC’s pied-a-terre tax notice rollout, finding no standing or injury. A separate challenge by Steve Wynn and Wilbur Ross to the underlying state law remains pending.  AI Summary

New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani’s administration won another round on the pied-à-terre tax Wednesday, when a state appeals court dismissed a homeowners’ lawsuit that had upended the surcharge’s rollout.

Appeals judges threw out an earlier Staten Island judge’s ruling ordering the city to cancel about 17,000 notices. They found the homeowners lacked standing and weren’t injured by the notices. All four judges agreed.

The surcharge is a pillar in the city’s plan to close a $12 billion budget gap. State officials projected it would raise about $500 million a year. The comptroller estimates the impact at closer to $1 billion before exemptions and appeals.

Wednesday’s decision does not directly affect a separate suit by billionaire casino magnate Steve Wynn, former Trump administration Commerce Secretary Wilbur Ross and his wife. They have challenged the state law behind the tax. That case remains pending in Suffolk County, with hearings scheduled in December.

A Staten Island judge had ruled Sept. 29 that the city skipped individualized decisions before mailing notices. The judge called the notices arbitrary and capricious and a due process violation.

Under the order, the Department of Finance was to cancel the notices and pull its roll from the agency website. New notices were allowed after an individualized review. Within hours, the city appealed and invoked a stay, so implementation continued.

Residential brokers told HousingWire TBD that the legal back-and-forth and the tax itself have had little impact on the owner-occupied market.

Ian Slater, co-founder and CEO of Trove Partners, said the market for under $5 million slowed in September and October. “We hit a record number of contract signatures above $5 million in September and October,” he said.

Wealth from a soaring stock market, plus nerves about its froth, is pushing buyers into top-end property, Slater said. Those buyers are “fully ignoring the pied-à-terre tax.” Some want second homes in New York City, he said.

However, listings of such properties remain scarce. Jessica Chestler, co-founder of The Chestler Jacobs Team at Douglas Elliman, said bidding wars have erupted. Supply isn’t keeping pace with buyer demand, including for homes priced above $5 million.

“We recently represented a buyer in a bidding war that we ultimately won, but it required going $1 million over asking on an apartment that needed a complete gut renovation,” Chestler said.

Because the homeowners lacked standing, the court never ruled on whether the NYC Department of Finance’s rollout was lawful. The court said other arguments were either without merit or unnecessary to resolve.

Finance later used 2025 tax data to revise its determinations, the court noted. Several hundred owners learned they did not owe the surcharge. The surcharge covers only non-primary residences. Through July 2028, the measure impacts houses worth $5 million or more. Co-ops and condos face a $1 million threshold.

Wynn and the Rosses sued in late September. They argue the surcharge is an illegal property tax that discriminates against nonresidents. Wynn’s notice totaled $183,094.69, while the Rosses’ notice came to $83,531.52.

Trove Partners’ Slater does not expect the climate to change. “Political winds are clearly against the wealthy class,” he said.

He doubts judges or politicians will move away from the tax. “It’s definitely an annoyance, but it’s a bit of much ado about nothing,” he said.

At MoMA, Brancusi Is Lost in Space

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The artist’s work is glorious and resonant, but the Museum of Modern Art has a space problem, or an elegance problem, or a crowd mitigation problem.

Let’s get one thing out of the way first: For all my weariness about how canonical dead White men are STILL. CONSISTENTLY. CENTERED in museum exhibitions — even after said museums have publicly taken pains to expand collections, hire curators with broad expertise and interests, and invite new audiences into their spaces — I have ZERO complaints about putting on a Constantin Brancusi exhibition. Even in fall 2026, when the city’s three major museums have turned themselves over to Brancusi, Lichtenstein, and Pollock simultaneously. (I will go to my grave insisting that it should have been a Krasner show.)

That said, the work of this particular canonical dead White man is glorious, and continues to resonate with contemporary artists: His name has come up consistently in interviews with sculptors this past year, whether Rodney McMillian or Martin Puryear or a number of others I’ve talked to. Brancusi: The Artist and His Studio at the Museum of Modern Art is a great reminder of why. More than a survey, it demonstrates the importance of this artist’s atelier as a place of creation, of exhibition, and of sociability, and includes his drawings, photographs, films, tools, and even some of the record albums he owned — reminders of the legendary dinner parties he used to throw there.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/providers/ardent-health-boosts-2026-guidance-volume-gains-expense-cuts" hreflang="en">Ardent Health boosts 2026 guidance on volume gains, expense cuts</a>

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →
Ardent Health boosts 2026 guidance on volume gains, expense cuts" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Ardent Health bumped up its 2026 earnings guidance as expense and efficiency improvements met solid “top of the funnel” demand for care during the third quarter, the for-profit shared this week.

The performance comes after a soft second quarter, with executives on Thursday’s earnings call noting that pain points like surgical volumes improved sequentially while broader challenges like Affordable Care Act marketplace dropoffs remain within projections.

Ardent’s shares were trading 20% above open as of Thursday afternoon in spite of Wall Street’s economy-wide selloff.

Leaders said that the company’s Project IMPACT (Improve Margins, Performance, Agility and Care Transformation) initiative is on track to hit “at least” its expected $70 million benefit for 2026, and highlighted a 4% year-over-year reduction in the quarter’s salaries, wages and benefits (SWB) expenses during the quarter driven in part by contract labor expense reduction.

Meanwhile, Ardent is reviewing its portfolio to determine which assets are a long-term strategic fit—leading to decisions like September’s divestiture from a joint venture with the University of Kansas Health System—and plans to deploy its capital toward organic growth in its mid-sized urban markets while maintaining a “disciplined approach” to acquisition or joint venture opportunities that prioritizes above-average potential market growth, CEO Dave Caspar told analysts Thursday.

“When we spoke with you last quarter, I outlined a clear set of priorities: deliver more consistent financial results, grow EBITDA, deploy capital effectively and execute against our commitments in a way that creates long-term shareholder value,” Caspar said during the earnings call. “… Taken together, our third-quarter performance and recent strategic portfolio optimization demonstrate meaningful progress against the priorities I outlined.”

For the third quarter, Ardent reported $1.62 billion in total revenue (3.1% year-over-year growth), $45 million of net income and $130 million in adjusted EBITDA.

The health system's admissions grew by 0.1% and its adjusted admissions by 2.9%, year-over-year improvements over the second quarter, with net patient service revenue per adjusted admission increasing 0.7% since Q3 2025. Inpatient surgeries dropped by 5% and outpatient surgeries by 0.6% compared to the prior-year period, with Chief Financial Officer Alfred Lumsdaine noting that about half of those drops were tied to the health system's "intentional rationalization of certain lower-margin service lines in targeted locations."

Executives said they were happy with the volumes, noting that a 7% drop in exchange admissions and corresponding self-pay increase, similar to Q2’s 8%, was “manageable and largely contemplated in our guidance.” They particularly pointed to strong showings at the top of their “demand funnel,” or areas like urgent care or physician visits that can lead to downstream utilization. Part of the company’s ongoing improvement efforts are focused on boosting access to these offerings while reducing “friction as we move patients through the journey” to capture more care.

“For us, there's a lot of we'll call it embedded growth or backed-up growth that we just need to unlock by reducing friction and ensuring that we're making the process of converting the top of the funnel to the bottom of the funnel easier,” Lumsdaine said.

On the expense side, executives said Project IMPACT helped pull SWB spending down to 39.9% of revenue during the quarter, a 300-basis-point improvement from last year, and slash contract labor from 3.5% of total SWB to 2%. Professional fee expense growth also declined from Q1 2026’s 12.9% to Q3’s 7.4%, with that slowdown expected to slow further in Q4. Supply growth was “a modest 2.6%,” per Lumsdaine.

The performance and expected efficiency gains led Ardent to adjust its full-year 2026 guidance. While revenues are still anticipated to land on “the lower end of our $6.4 billion to $6.7 billion range,” the target for adjusted EBIDTA rose 1.5% at the midpoint.

On expectations for 2027, they declined to outline any specific number targets but noted well-recognized industry headwinds like Medicaid work requirements, marketplace plan attrition, as well as tailwinds like the Project IMPACT performance improvements and the recently finalized outpatient payment rule.

“I'm extremely proud of the progress our team has made this year, despite very fluid industry and macroeconomic dynamics,” Lumsdaine said. “We continue to strengthen our operating model, and we remain focused on building on our performance, generating strong cash flow and creating long-term shareholder value by aligning our people, capital and assets with the highest value opportunities.”

Ardent Health’s operations include 30 acute care hospitals and over 280 care sites, several of which are run as part of a joint venture structure with other health system partners. In 2025, the company increased its revenues by 6% to $6.3 billion and grew its adjusted EBITDA to $545 million.

Zipline hits $21.5B valuation as Walmart expands bet on drone delivery

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Zipline has raised $1.5 billion and ramped up its partnership with Walmart, a pair of deals that have raised the autonomous drone delivery and logistics startup’s profile and valuation.

Zipline and Walmart reached a new multi-year agreement to add 300 drone delivery sites across the United States. The two companies have had a drone delivery agreement since 2021, but this deal is a significant jump from the 21 active drone delivery sites they have together today.

On the fundraising side, Zipline’s $1.5 billion raise was led by Paradigm, WCM Investment Management, and Layer Global, with participation from Valor Equity Partners, Fidelity Management & Research Company, Uber, Tiger Global, J.P. Morgan, ARK Invest, and Salesforce Ventures. Zipline is now valued at $21.5 billion.

The company, which has developed a drone delivery ecosystem that includes aircraft, launch and landing systems, and logistics software, said it plans to use the funds to expand its U.S. manufacturing facilities and hire more workers to build out a nationwide drone delivery network.

Founded in 2014, Zipline got its start in Africa, where it used its autonomous drones to deliver blood in Rwanda, and later expanded to Ghana and Nigeria. The California-based company began its first operations in the U.S. in 2020, ferrying medical equipment and supplies to Novant Health in North Carolina. The following year, it struck a deal with Walmart.

But it was in 2022 that Zipline’s business really started to take off in the United States. Zipline received its Part 135 air carrier certification from the Federal Aviation Administration that year, a critical regulatory hurdle that authorized the company to complete long-range commercial drone deliveries. It has locked in a number of partnerships since then, including with Uber earlier this year.

Zipline now uses its drones to deliver food, retail, agricultural, and health products in five African countries, several cities in the United States, and Japan. Zipline’s home delivery service uses Zipline’s Platform 2 drones, which are designed to carry up to eight pounds and travel to customers within a 10-mile radius. Its larger Platform 1 drones are used for long-range deliveries for enterprise, business, and government that can cover 120 miles round trip.

The company said Thursday that its share of delivery revenue grew from 10.8% to 34.9% between March and October of this year. This latest funding aims to push that metric even higher.

These Wine Glasses Are the Only Ones That Can Survive My Clumsiness

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

I spent years living with roommates and making due with our collective assortment of mismatched dishware, throwing washed take-out containers haphazardly into a cupboard and reusing them until they were cracked and stained, and living with a pantry that was in a constant state of moderate disarray. But when I moved in with my boyfriend, I took the opportunity to get my shit together. I pride myself on being a relatively put-together person, and that now translates to my kitchen too. I’ve got a cabinet full of well-organized food storage containers with their corresponding lids, I keep my spices neatly stacked in deli containers with labeled with color-coded kitchen tape, I replaced my chipped water glasses with a matching set, and I have a special drawer that houses the linens, taper candles, and flatware I pull out when I’m hosting. It all feels very adult.

Until recently, though, there was one remaining aspect of my kitchen that still needed help. While I have more fun coupes and rocks glasses than I know what to do with, I haven’t owned a matching set of wine glasses since college—in fact, at the time of my move, I had two left to my name. It’s not for lack of trying: I’ve tested out glassware during my time at BA, bringing home countless options in the hopes that they’d be my forever stems. I’ve somehow managed to break every single one of them, which has become a running joke for those that know me.

But despite my clumsiness, I’m not willing to sacrifice style for durability. I want a thin-stemmed, thin-rimmed, elegant wine glass that won’t snap in two the minute I lay my hands on it. Is that too much to ask?

For the longest time, I thought it might be, and had resigned myself to sipping wine out of stemless tumblers—until I discovered Gabriel-Glas’s StandArt All Purpose Wine Glass. They’ve got a sleek, slightly conical shape that I find enhances reds, whites, and rosés alike, with a slight curve between the bowl and the rim that adds a touch of personality to an otherwise classic silhouette. The stem is thin enough to satisfy my desire for elegance without being so delicate that I’m scared I’ll snap it, and the laser-cut rim is a joy to sip from.

Because the glasses are machine-blown from a single piece of molten crystal, they have none of the weak points that tend to form where traditional multipart glasses are joined. The machine-blown construction also keeps the cost per glass low enough that I won’t mind replacing one if (let’s be real, when) I do drop and shatter it. But I’m pleased to report that as of right now, all six of my beloved StandArt glasses are intact and ready to take on my new apartment’s first season of holiday hosting and cozy weather hangs. Bring on the winter reds and festive bottles of bubbly.

Core Scientific (CORZ) to Exit Bitcoin Mining by Year-End as Colocation Hits $215 Million

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Core Scientific will complete its exit from bitcoin mining by year-end and enter 2027 as a pure-play high-density colocation provider, management said on Thursday’s third-quarter earnings call, capping a pivot that has already cut the company’s mining fleet nearly in half.

Chief Financial Officer Jim Nygaard told investors the company expects to “complete the wind-down during the fourth quarter.” Chief Executive Officer Adam Sullivan put the same end date more bluntly: “We expect to complete our exit from Bitcoin mining by year end.” The comments landed alongside a results release filed with the SEC that shows how far the shift has already gone.

Colocation revenue climbed nearly 60% sequentially to $215.4 million in the quarter ended Sept. 30, the earnings exhibit shows. Digital asset self-mining revenue fell to $12.3 million, down about 79% from $57.4 million a year earlier. Hosted mining for third parties added just $5.2 million. Of $232.9 million in total revenue, colocation now accounts for roughly 92%.

Self-mining posted a $7.5 million gross loss in the quarter. Colocation gross profit was $128.4 million, a 60% margin. Adjusted EBITDA more than tripled sequentially to $133.6 million, with the margin expanding to 57% from 25%.

Nygaard said Core Scientific had nearly 50% fewer miners online at the end of September than at year-end 2025. The company has been repurposing power and floor space under long-term customer agreements for AI and high-performance computing workloads, a process its investor materials describe as continuing “as circumstances allow.”

That path is not unique among public miners. Peers have been selling bitcoin and leasing campuses to AI tenants, and research notes have flagged a widening funding gap between those that can finance data center builds and those that cannot.

Core Scientific is billing for 485 megawatts of colocation capacity as of Sept. 30 — up 90 MW in the quarter — representing more than $700 million in average annualized colocation GAAP revenue. The remaining roughly 100 MW under its CoreWeave buildout is in commissioning and expected to start recognizing revenue over the next few months, Sullivan said.

Beyond CoreWeave’s contracted 590 MW, Core Scientific has 530 MW under an AMD-backed agreement across five campuses, with initial delivery targeted for the first half of 2027 and full delivery by the end of 2028. Together, the two relationships cover about 1.1 GW. Once fully delivered and billing, management expects that portfolio alone to generate $1.8 billion in average annual run-rate revenue and $1.6 billion in net operating income.

Bitcoin is almost gone from the books. Digital assets stood at $2.7 million on Sept. 30, down from $49.7 million three months earlier and $222 million at year-end 2025. Earlier this year, Core Scientific was still adding coins even as it sold into the pivot; that chapter is effectively closed.

The mining exit has already carried a cash cost. A $41.9 million loss on contract termination booked in the second quarter reflects the decision to end a Block/Proto mining chip supply agreement. Liquidity ended the third quarter at about $1.7 billion, including roughly $800 million in cash, after heavy capital spending on AMD sites and the Polaris acquisition.

Sullivan framed the wind-down as simplifying the business toward “more attractive long-term growth opportunities.” Nygaard added that starting 2027 with a “very clean P&L” — one without mining in the numbers — should give investors a clearer look at a true colocation run-rate.

The test now is whether Core Scientific turns the last machines off on schedule in the fourth quarter, and whether the AMD financing package lands in time to keep that clean P&L growing.

Why Your Seatback Screen May Be Dark On Your Next United Airlines Flight

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

If you board a United Airlines flight and find a placard covering an inoperative seatback screen, it may be a consequence of an upgrade rather than an unexpected malfunction. United is installing Starlink ahead of cabin renovations on some aircraft, leaving passengers with excellent Wi-Fi but no working seatback entertainment.

An internal United memo reviewed by Live And Let’s Fly, explains that a small number of Boeing 737-900 aircraft, identified internally as the 73C configuration, have received Starlink before their United Next interior refresh.

That installation has deactivated the existing DirecTV system. The result is that the old seatback monitors no longer function.

In other words, the screen in front of you is still there, but your entertainment will need to come from your own device…at least temporarily.

> Read More: United Airlines Will Modernize Mainline Jets With New Interiors

The memo identifies these five aircraft by United’s internal fleet numbers:

That is the list as of November 3, rather than a permanent or comprehensive list of aircraft that may undergo this transition.

United says affected screens carry placards explaining the entertainment upgrade and directing MileagePlus members to free Starlink Wi-Fi. The aircraft also have Starlink signage beside the boarding door.

With the screens disabled, flight attendants must perform a live safety demonstration instead of playing the safety video.

I’d trade Starlink for DirecTV any day of the week and twice on Sundays…

United’s Starlink rollout is accelerating. Speaking at the CyberSat conference this week, United Chief Information Security Officer Deneen DeFiore said the deployment timeline had been reduced from two to three years to 18 months.

As of November 5, United’s official Starlink tracker lists 677 equipped aircraft and says Starlink is available on 50% of its daily flights. The tracker continues to list completion by the end of 2027.

You can also use that tool to check an upcoming flight within three days of departure. Aircraft assignments can change, so check again closer to travel.

The five aircraft in the memo illustrate a consequence of moving the connectivity installation ahead of the interior work but considering you can stream TV from your phones or computers I don’t see it as a loss of any sort.

I would much rather have fast, reliable, free internet than an aging DirecTV system. For working onboard, Starlink is a far more useful amenity to me.

That calculus changes slightly when traveling with my children. They don’t have phones so the setback monitors are very helpful to keeping them occupied during the flight. Having internet on your phone also does not replace the convenience of watching something on the screen in front of you while using that phone for something else.

But there are just growing pains in the ex-DirecTV fleet and I would not want United to delay Starlink simply to keep the old television system running. Still, passengers should know what to expect. Bring a charged device and headphones if you plan to watch something.

Some United Boeing 737-900s now have Starlink but inoperative seatback screens because the connectivity installation preceded their United Next cabin refresh. The long-term improvement is welcome. In the meantime, enjoy the better Wi-Fi, but expect to supply your own screen on these aircraft.

The Onion’s new plan to market its print newspaper: sponsoring a bowl game

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

All the latest headlines and insights from the world of digital journalism and publishing. Our daily newsletter is a must-read from newsrooms to executive suites.

We will never sell your address. Prefer a weekly email?

We have sent a confirmation link to . Click it and you are on the list. If it has not turned up in a minute or two, it may be in your spam folder.

College football already has an Orange Bowl, a Peach Bowl, and a Citrus Bowl, so on its face, there’s nothing outlandish about the concept of an Onion Bowl.

But this matchup wouldn’t be titled to evoke some locale’s bumper crop, à la the Famous Idaho Potato Bowl. Instead it would market “America’s Finest News Source,” The Onion. (Today’s top headlines: Bain Capital Acquires Tibetan Buddhism For $35 Million; Abdul El-Sayed Makes History As First Physically Healthy Senator; Chuck Schumer Delivers Victory Speech To Previously Enthusiastic Crowd.)

On Thursday morning, Onion CEO (and past Nieman Lab contributor) Ben Collins announced the publication’s quest to buy naming rights for a bowl game, both on Bluesky and on the podcast Pablo Torre Finds Out:

The Onion is extremely close to buying an honest-to-goodness, Division 1 college football bowl series bowl game.

Yes, we're trying to pull off The Onion Bowl.

A few days before Christmas, potentially in Texas.

But we've already been banned once. And we need your help.

— Ben Collins (@bencollins.bsky.social) November 5, 2026 at 8:32 AM

For those unfamiliar, American college football seasons traditionally end with a few dozen bowl games, scattered around the country (and occasionally outside it). Nearly all of these games sell naming rights to one company or another, leading to fun combinations like the Poulan Weed Eater Independence Bowl, the TaxSlayer.com Gator Bowl, and the Union Home Mortgage Gasparilla Bowl. Collins wants to buy the rights to one of the several bowl games that are still without a sponsor for their games next month.

This absurdity — though no more absurd, I suppose, than the Snoop Dogg Arizona Bowl presented by Gin & Juice by Dre and Snoop — serves as the excellent marketing hook to get people to buy a $75/year print subscription, a major driver of The Onion’s revenue.

Indeed, only three U.S. newspapers have a daily print circulation over 100,000 — The Wall Street Journal, The New York Times, and the New York Post; The Washington Post and USA Today have already fallen below that line. (At the risk of being pedantic, though, The Onion appears in print monthly, not daily, making it a bit of an apples-to-oranges comparison. Also, The Onion gets to make up its stories.)

Collins says The Onion actually got quite close to buying the naming rights to the New Orleans Bowl, which last year lost its longtime naming sponsor, the…Ohio-based freight-shipping concern R+L Carriers? The Onion was prepared to pay “mid six figures.” But the organization that runs the bowl, the Greater New Orleans Sports Foundation, apparently said no deep into the process, a move Collins attributed to “politics.” (I reached out to the Greater New Orleans Sports Foundation for comment — a sentence I did not expect to type today — but did not hear back.)

So the guys who sell bowl games ushered us to what we thought was a pretty good fit: The New Orleans Bowl.

I love New Orleans. I love having crawfish out of the back of a pickup truck at 3 a.m. The game would've been in the Superdome two days before Christmas. Good fit! Right? Right???

— Ben Collins (@bencollins.bsky.social) November 5, 2026 at 8:38 AM

Then the folks who run the New Orleans Bowl, a commission that includes representatives of the city and state of Louisiana, found out our plans to highlight our mascot:

— Ben Collins (@bencollins.bsky.social) November 5, 2026 at 8:39 AM

Despite getting all approvals from ESPN and scraping together the cash, right as we were about to sign the paper work to put The Onion Sports Bowl in New Orleans, their state-funded commission called us and said it straight up: "No."

We asked why. They said politics. And the Sickos Guy.

— Ben Collins (@bencollins.bsky.social) November 5, 2026 at 8:40 AM

Electric Buses Need a Plan for the Next Operating Contract

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Greater Manchester is entering a new stage of bus franchising. In September 2026, Go-Ahead announced that Go North West had won two Bee Network operating contracts covering 38 routes and approximately 60 buses, with service beginning in March 2027. Eighteen of those routes will operate from a new depot in Leigh. The award is a reminder that operating companies can change while the depots, charging infrastructure and grid connections supporting the passenger network remain in place for decades.

Electric-bus procurement adds a long-lived physical commitment to contracts that are often much shorter. An operating agreement may run for five to seven years. A depot building, substation or multi-megawatt electricity connection can remain useful through several such contracts. Buses can sometimes be transferred between operators, but land, cable routes, electrical capacity and installed charging equipment are tied to particular sites. Those assets need to support the passenger service even when the company responsible for driving the buses changes.

Consider an authority retendering electric-bus routes. The incumbent has used a convenient depot and financed chargers and a substantial grid connection. A competing operator submits an attractive price and a credible operating plan, but has no assured right to use the depot and electricity infrastructure. It might need to negotiate with the incumbent, find alternative premises or secure another grid connection, potentially adding years of work and substantial costs. A nominally competitive tender can become much less contestable once those obligations are included.

South Yorkshire has taken a deliberate approach to this problem. In March 2026, the South Yorkshire Mayoral Combined Authority announced the purchase of five major depots, adding them to the publicly owned Leger Way depot in Doncaster. First Bus and Stagecoach will lease the acquired premises back during the transition. The authority intends to use the estate for franchised operations and electric-bus infrastructure, with its first franchised services scheduled for September 2027. Six operators reached the next procurement stage for the initial packages at Olive Grove and Leger Way.

This does not establish that every city should own every depot. London has long maintained a publicly specified, competitively operated bus network while many garages remain outside direct Transport for London ownership. Secure leases, access rights, assignable contracts and specialist infrastructure providers may preserve the same continuity, depending on the local market. The practical test is whether a qualified successor operator can obtain the depot, buses, charging capacity and operational information required to take over on the contracted date.

Remaining asset value is central to that test. If an operator finances twenty-year charging infrastructure under a five-year contract, it needs to know what happens to the investment if another operator wins the routes. The authority must also avoid paying for a useful electrical connection and then finding it cannot make that capacity available to the next contractor. Depreciation, compensation, asset transfer, warranties, software access and grid-connection permissions need to be settled before the first contract ends, ideally before it starts.

England’s January 2026 bus-franchising guidance recognizes the importance of depots, charging infrastructure, transfer arrangements and competition in subsequent procurements. The engineering of electric buses has advanced considerably. Public authorities now need procurement structures that keep the fleet and its supporting infrastructure useful as routes, contractors and technologies evolve.

My TFIE Strategy Briefing article examines the second-contract test through Greater Manchester and South Yorkshire, including asset access, residual values and operating continuity. It draws on my open report, The Bus Is Not the System, comparing metropolitan bus governance and electrification across Britain and Ireland.

Expedia CEO says AI agents aren't changing booking habits — yet

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Expedia CEO Ariane Gorin said Thursday the company hasn't yet seen artificial intelligence agents affecting the way customers book, but the early signs show potential for an industry shift.

"It's a great way for people to do inspiration, to do trip planning," Gorin said in an interview with CNBC. "We haven't seen it change the way they're actually doing booking."

The comments come as personal AI agents like Meta's Muse and OpenAI's Dots enter the mainstream, promising to tackle everything from scheduling to shopping for everyday Americans. Opportunities in travel planning and booking could pose a competitive threat to traditional sites like Expedia.

Gorin said Expedia is "working with all of the agents" to make sure it surfaces in AI results, to understand how the agents get information and to make sure that the company has the necessary integrations.

She acknowledged it's possible that AI agents could develop their own travel programs and eventually claim market share, but she said complexities within the industry — customer service, for example — would be difficult to fully replace with artificial intelligence.

"People were already shopping across a number of places, so the agent can do that in a much easier way," Gorin said. "But when it comes to actually booking — being confident that the content that you're getting is right, the amenities are right, the price is right, if something goes wrong, someone's going to actually either solve it for you, answer the phone, be there for you — I don't think those agents are going to do it for you, and that's why people trust Expedia and come to us."

Shares of Expedia were down about 12% Thursday after the company delivered weaker-than-expected guidance for the fourth quarter on Wednesday. The company forecast revenue of between $3.69 billion and $3.75 billion for the final three months of the year, making the midpoint lower than analyst estimates of $3.74 billion, according to FactSet.

Gorin attributed the "moderation" in Expedia's fourth-quarter guidance to "geopolitical events in the Middle East and Europe," the midterm elections and the company moving its fall sale from the fourth quarter to the third quarter. But she noted the company reported a strong third quarter, as total gross bookings rose 11% and revenue climbed 12%.

Blues have won four of their last five Championship matches and sit third in the table ahead of the final round of fixtures before the two-week international break, with Swansea only two places below on goal difference.

Davies isn't exactly short of options at this moment in time, although James Beadle and August Priske were omitted from the matchday squad against Millwall through injury.

Stanley Tucci Reveals His One Holiday Tablescape Rule That’s Not What You’d Expect

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

His holiday palette surprisingly restrained.

Hosting on an ordinary night can be stressful, but holiday hosting takes it to another level. Between curating the perfect themes, taking time to honor every tradition, and packing the schedule full of activities, it’s easy to muddle your aesthetic amid all that prescribed cheer.

Luckily, there are easy fixes. Actor, filmmaker, and trendsetter Stanley Tucci recently sat down with House Beautiful to share his top tips for hosting with style. His holiday hot take? One traditional holiday hue should play a supporting role, not take center stage.

“I love autumnal colors, so I do that, obviously, a lot. We'll do that for Thanksgiving, then Christmas will be more white and green, with some red, but not too much,” says Tucci.

An arbitrator of taste on and off-screen, Tucci believes the only place red hues should take on the holiday table is in their natural habitat, perched on a branch.

“Berries on branches. That's all the red you need,” he says.

Tucci describes his hosting style as classic, simple, and timeless. From the menu to the tablecloth, he steers clear of flashy, overdone clichés—which is reason enough for you to ignore the trend cycle this holiday season (looking at you, Ralph Lauren Christmas), and decorate with what you already own and love.

This year marks the fifth anniversary of Tucci’s partnership with S.Pellegrino, which began with an annual limited-edition bottle featuring his engraved signature. The line has since expanded to include “Dinner Dialogues,” with conversation starters on each label. The 2026 collection continues the concept, with designs inspired by Tucci’s minimalist, curated style. It features three bespoke labels—and the actor admits he has a favorite.

“‘What memory makes you smile?’ I love this one,” Tucci says.

With a little help from Tucci, your table’s style—and the conversation around it—will be elevated and effortless.

St. Mary and the shrinking reach of Smith

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Type to search articles, cases, and authors.

Justice Clarence Thomas began Colorado’s half of the argument in St. Mary Catholic Parish v. Roy with a pointed observation. In the court’s last two cases about religious schools and public benefits, Espinoza v. Montana Department of Revenue and Carson v. Makin, the justices “did not use, even cite, Smith.” So, he asked Colorado’s deputy solicitor general, “Why would we do that in this case?”

That question goes to the heart of the case. After argument, it would be surprising if Colorado won. The real fight is over how the Catholic preschools win. And the answer will determine how much is left of Employment Division v. Smith, perhaps the court’s most contested free exercise precedent.

In 2020, Colorado voters approved funding for universal preschool. The program that followed pays for 15 hours a week of preschool for every four-year-old in the state, worth about $6,300 per child. Families can spend that money at roughly 2,000 participating preschools: public, private, and religious. Every provider must accept an “equal opportunity” mandate. Specifically, children must have an equal opportunity to enroll “regardless of race, ethnicity, religious affiliation, sexual orientation, gender identity, lack of housing, income level, or disability.”

The mandate is not absolute. Colorado lets preschools use enrollment “preferences” for, among other things, children of employees, siblings, members of a specific community, and families willing to volunteer. More than 1,000 preschools use at least one. But Catholic parish preschools in the Archdiocese of Denver ask enrolling families to support the Church’s teachings on sex and marriage, and they treat children according to their biological sex. Families must affirm those teachings in good faith. Colorado says those practices violate the mandate, so the parishes cannot participate. Families who want a Catholic preschool lose the benefit. Petitioners Daniel and Lisa Sheley have lost more than $12,000 so far. The lower courts largely sided with Colorado.

The free exercise clause bars the government from “prohibiting the free exercise” of religion. For decades, the court read that guarantee to require strict scrutiny of laws that substantially burden religious practice. In 1963’s Sherbert v. Verner, South Carolina denied unemployment benefits to a Seventh-day Adventist who would not work on her Sabbath. The court held that the state could not force her to choose between her faith and a public benefit without a compelling reason.

Smith changed course in 1990. Oregon had denied unemployment benefits to two men fired for using peyote in a Native American Church ceremony. Writing for the court, Justice Antonin Scalia held that a neutral law of general applicability (that is, one that neither targets religious conduct nor exempts comparable secular conduct) needs only a rational basis, however heavily it burdens religious exercise. Under that rule, the government almost always wins.

But Smith did not overrule Sherbert. It distinguished it, and the court has spent the decades since narrowing Smith’s reach. Petitioners count eight categories of free exercise cases in which the court has declined to apply it: animus toward a religious group or person (Masterpiece Cakeshop); church autonomy (Hosanna-Tabor); discrimination among religions (Larson v. Valente); forced participation in ceremonies (Barnette); hybrid rights, which pair free exercise with another constitutional right (Cantwell); parental rights over children’s religious upbringing (Mahmoud v. Taylor); penalties on religious belief as such (McDaniel v. Paty); and the receipt of public benefits (from Sherbert through Carson). Even within Smith’s domain, a law gets strict scrutiny if it is not truly “neutral” or “generally applicable,” for example because it allows individualized exemptions (Fulton) or treats comparable secular conduct more favorably (Tandon).

The public benefits category is where Carson comes in. In three cases over five years, the court held that states may not exclude religious schools from otherwise available funding. 2017’s Trinity Lutheran involved a church preschool barred from a playground-resurfacing grant. 2020’s Espinoza involved a scholarship program closed to religious schools. And in Carson, decided in 2022, Maine paid tuition at private schools but excluded schools that taught religion. The court said all of these limits violated the free exercise clause. “Regardless of how the benefit and restriction are described,” it explained, “the program operates to identify and exclude otherwise eligible schools on the basis of their religious exercise.” Its decisions, the court said, “turned on the substance of free exercise protections, not on the presence or absence of magic words.”

The Catholic preschools offer the court two routes to strict scrutiny in this case. They track the two questions the court agreed to decide.

The first route stays inside Smith. Colorado’s mandate, petitioners argue, is not generally applicable. The state lets more than 1,000 preschools limit enrollment for secular reasons but refuses to accommodate the parishes’ religious ones. The U.S. Court of Appeals for the 10th Circuit rejected that argument, holding that a challenger must show unfettered discretion or categorical exemptions for identical secular conduct.

The second route goes around Smith. Under Carson, the preschools argue, strict scrutiny applies whenever the government conditions a generally available benefit on giving up religious exercise, whether or not the condition is neutral. Colorado, for its part, reads Carson far more narrowly, as a rule against excluding schools because they are religious. Colorado does not exclude religious preschools as such, and many participate. On that view, Carson does not apply and Smith does.

The choice between those routes is where the real stakes lie. A win on general applicability would be narrow and fragile. It would turn on the details of Colorado’s exemptions. Colorado, or any other state, could respond by trimming its secular exemptions and excluding the same schools again. The next challenger would have to start over, hunting for a new secular comparator. Principal Deputy Solicitor General Sarah Harris called that regime “an endless sort of whack-a-mole game.”

A win under Carson, in contrast, would be far more durable. It would hold that conditioning a public benefit on abandoning religious exercise triggers strict scrutiny, however the condition is drafted. That rule would stop the kind of maneuvering Maine engaged in after Carson itself, when it rewrote its program to exclude the same religious schools without using religious labels. And it would take an entire domain, public benefits, out of Smith’s reach.

That is why Thomas’ question, which this article began with, mattered. Going in, some commentators wondered whether the court would use this case to bury Smith. CNN’s Joan Biskupic framed it that way. The argument suggested something more measured, though still significant. If the court does not overrule Smith here, it seems poised to quarantine it as far as it can.

Here’s one version of what that quarantine could look like. The court can read Carson’s public benefits rule for what it says. It can apply strict scrutiny, properly understood, in that domain. It can ground that choice in the clause’s original meaning. And it can say plainly what the United States told the court: Smith is the anomaly.

Justice Neil Gorsuch asked what Colorado’s narrow reading would leave of a decade of precedent. On Colorado’s view, he wondered, had the court been “just wasting our time for the last 10 years” on Carson,Trinity Lutheran, and Espinoza? If so, any state could escape those cases by redrafting.

Indeed, states are doing exactly that. Petitioners’ counsel, Nick Reaves, told Gorsuch that “before the ink was even dry in Carson, Maine changed its laws” so that they “don’t explicitly call out religion, but they still exclude the same families and the same schools.” Colorado’s own record shows the same pattern. The state amended its discretionary enrollment exemption process during trial. More than once, it redefined an exemption that originally allowed faith-based preschools to limit enrollment to members of their congregation. Then, while the case was on appeal, it repealed that congregation exemption entirely and argued that the repeal freed it from the district court’s injunction.

Reading Carson for what it says would not create a boundless exception. Reaves located the boundary in Smith’s own concern. Smith “was concerned about every man becoming a law unto himself,” he said, and about “broad exemptions to criminal prohibitions. That interest is not as strong when it comes to public benefits when the underlying conduct is lawful.” When Justice Amy Coney Barrett asked whether a law making the mandate a condition of every preschool’s license to operate would fall under Carson, Reaves conceded, “I don’t think that would be a Carson case.”

Harris marked a second boundary. Justice Sonia Sotomayor asked about Bowen v. Roy, a 1986 decision rejecting a Native American father’s religious objection to the government’s own use of his daughter’s Social Security number. Harris pointed to the part of Bowen that commanded a majority: “You can’t force a change in the way in which the government itself manages internal operations.” Government conduct that neither pressures nor prohibits religious exercise triggers no scrutiny, as Michalyn Steele and I have explained elsewhere about Bowen. In sum, Carson’s rule has limits on both sides. It reaches conditions on public benefits for lawful conduct, not licensing regimes or the government’s management of its own affairs.

The natural worry is what comes next. If strict scrutiny governs benefits conditions, will every program become a negotiation with every objector? Justice Elena Kagan pressed this concern. Everything, she said, would turn on whether a law passes “strict scrutiny, which is a kind of balancing test, which is like how important is the state interest? How important is the religious belief? How do we compare the one to the other?”

But that is a caricature of strict scrutiny, at least as the court applies it in this context. Gorsuch offered the more accurate account, and Harris confirmed it. “[W]e assess whether the government has a compelling interest and whether it’s narrowly tailored to that interest without balancing against the interest on the other side,” Gorsuch said. Once a claimant shows a substantial burden, “[w]e don’t go balancing the two.” Gorsuch is exactly right about that.

I do not deny that older strict scrutiny opinions sometimes used the vocabulary of balancing. But the doctrine has since been disciplined, largely through the courts’ experience with the Religious Freedom Restoration Act and the Religious Land Use and Institutionalized Persons Act, passed in Smith’s wake. Courts applying RFRA and RLUIPA do not ask how central a belief is to a faith. They ask a threshold question about whether the government has objectively burdened religious exercise. At the scrutiny stage, they do not weigh the value of religion against the value of a policy. As I have argued elsewhere, strict scrutiny works as an exclusionary norm paired with a heavy evidentiary burden. It excludes all but a narrow set of permissible reasons for burdening religious exercise. Then it requires the government to prove that its action is necessary to serve one of them. That’s precisely what Gorsuch described.

The court has also adopted this disciplined approach in other contexts. In United States v. Hemani last term, it held that the government cannot disarm a person merely by naming a permissible reason. It must prove that the reason is one it actually acts on, and tie that reason to the person. As I wrote at the time, history fixed the permissible ends, and the court then demanded proof that the means served them. That is how constitutional rights work more generally: as constraints on the reasons government may act on. Strict scrutiny does not ask how much religion matters. It asks whether the state’s reason is one the Constitution permits, and whether the state can prove that reason applies to this claimant.

Harris invoked Gonzales v. O Centro, the court’s leading RFRA strict scrutiny precedent, for this very point. “I think O Centro rightly recognized strict scrutiny,” she said. “[E]ven when people say it’s tough, it is a very manageable standard for courts. It’s a familiar standard for courts.” O Centro requires the government to show that its interest is served by applying the law “to the person,” meaning “the particular claimant whose sincere exercise of religion is being substantially burdened.” Courts must look “beyond broadly formulated interests” to the harm of granting the specific exemption requested.

The track record bears this out. Gorsuch noted that the federal government “already has to meet strict scrutiny” in providing its many public benefits. Harris pointed to the federal experience under RFRA and to “the 40 states that have RFRA equivalents.” Under the pre-Smith regime, she added, cases “turned out both ways.” Public schools already operate under this regime. The District of Columbia’s schools are subject to federal RFRA. As Gorsuch put it, Senators Ted Kennedy and Orrin Hatch “would be quite surprised to think that RFRA created laws unto themselves in every American.”

Smith, by contrast, is the unstable regime. The court’s cases have left what Harris called “a mess,” in which “it’s hard to describe Smith as a default, and it’s even harder to describe Smith as a workable default in this particular context.” Strict scrutiny is the familiar standard. Smith, in her words, is “a much less stable jurisprudence.”

Restoring strict scrutiny for benefits conditions would also move free exercise doctrine closer to the clause’s original meaning. Two justices asked whether strict scrutiny is faithful to that meaning, and both counsel answered yes.

Barrett asked Reaves whether strict scrutiny is “what you think is required by the original meaning.” Reaves answered that Sherbert v. Verner is “much closer to the original meaning than Smith.” He pointed to the “peace and safety provisos” in founding-era state constitutions, which protected religious exercise unless it threatened public peace or safety. Barrett then put the point precisely: “So you see it as an implementing doctrine that’s close to how it would have been implemented at the time of the founding?” “I think that’s right, yes,” Reaves said. Gorsuch asked Harris a parallel question. Is strict scrutiny, as the court announced it in Sherbert and Thomas v. Review Board and applied it in the recent benefits cases, “a reasonable construction of … the original meaning?” Harris said yes.

That framing is right. Strict scrutiny is not itself the original meaning. It is a doctrine for implementing that meaning, a form of what originalists call constitutional construction. Mark Storslee and I made this argument in our amicus brief in the case, which petitioners’ counsel also cited at argument for the history of Catholic exclusion from the common schools. The brief draws on my work elsewhere, including Constructing Constitutional Rights and Replacing Smith.

The core of the argument is this. The founding generation did not let religious exercise yield to any reason government happened to articulate. Early state constitutions took some justifications for burdening religious exercise off the table. Others, most commonly the protection of public peace and safety, were permissible. And founding-era courts made the government show that it was actually advancing one of those permissible interests before religious exercise had to give way. For example, in People v. Philips, decided in 1813, a New York court refused to force a Catholic priest to testify about a confession. Compelling him, the court explained, would place him “between Scylla and Charybdis,” and the priest’s silence posed no threat to the peace or safety the state constitution protected. That is what strict scrutiny requires: a permissible reason, proven on the facts.

The evidence from Reconstruction points the same way. The 14th Amendment, which applies the free exercise clause to the states, was framed by a generation shaped by religious resistance to the Fugitive Slave Act. Abolitionist believers insisted on a right to give a fugitive shelter and a “crust of bread,” whatever neutral and generally applicable law said. As Kurt Lash and I have explained, that understanding informed the amendment’s privileges or immunities clause. Judge Ryan Nelson of the 9th Circuit relied on that history in a recent concurrence concluding that Smith “breaks from the history and tradition surrounding free exercise and the Fourteenth Amendment.” At argument, Harris agreed with Justice Brett Kavanaugh that, as a matter of original meaning and contrary to Smith, the free exercise clause is “more than simply a protection against explicit discrimination.”

One serious question about the Carson approach came from Kavanaugh. A rule that applies strict scrutiny to benefits conditions, but not to outright bans, would impose “greater First Amendment scrutiny on a benefits program than … an outright ban on the religious conduct.” That, he said, “seems inverted.” On petitioners’ theory, a state that merely withholds funding would face strict scrutiny, while a state that banned the same conduct outright, through a neutral law, would get only Smith’s rational basis review. He acknowledged one answer, that the court “should just overrule Smith entirely,” but asked Reaves to assume that was off the table.

The incoherence is real. And if the court doesn’t overrule Smith in this case, hopefully it will take that step someday soon. But the incoherence belongs to Smith itself. Harris put it directly: “Smith is the anomaly in the context of public benefits or in any other context, where other rights do not have this so long as a law is generally applicable rational basis review.”

Smith itself claimed the opposite, calling religious exemptions “a constitutional anomaly.” As Mark Rienzi and I have argued, the reverse is true. Exemptions are a species of as-applied challenge, the default remedy elsewhere in constitutional law. The anomaly is Smith’s rule, not the exemptions it disfavors.

The court has retired other precedents it no longer believes in. It distinguishes them, whittles them away, and finally overrules them. Teague v. Lane’s “watershed” exception for new rules of criminal procedure went unused for 32 years before Edwards v. Vannoy declared it “moribund” and eliminated it. Gorsuch joined that opinion. Abood v. Detroit Board of Education was cabined inHarris v. Quinn before Janus v. AFSCME overruled it. Lemon v. Kurtzman was sidestepped for years before Kennedy v. Bremerton confirmed that the court had “long ago abandoned” it.

After decades of whittling, quarantine is the penultimate stage of that life cycle. The court can further limit Smith’s damage now. Eventually the court will likely complete the life cycle and retire Smith altogether.

The most striking moments in the argument were strict scrutiny questions in all but name. Justice Samuel Alito asked how many times the feared harm had actually happened, and observed that there was “little evidence of a practical problem.” Barrett asked whether there was any record of LGBTQ parents being turned away from these preschools, and whether any entity other than religious ones had sought an exemption from the mandate. “No, Your Honor,” Colorado’s counsel answered. Kavanaugh asked the state, “Why not seek the win/win?” With roughly 2,000 preschools in the program and 34 Catholic ones, he saw “plenty of opportunities to respect the rights of the religious believers and respect the ability of LGBT parents to find” a school that fits. “Why has this been provoked?” he asked.

Those are the questions narrow tailoring forces a government to answer. Strict scrutiny asks whether the state can prove that its reason applies here, not merely name it. That demand makes room for the win-win Kavanaugh described. Religious preschools can serve the families who want them, and every family can find a school that fits. Smith invites the opposite: winner-take-all conflicts provoked by a rule that never asks whether the conflict was necessary.

The best case for quarantining Smith is not that religious claimants deserve to win. It is that strict scrutiny asks the questions this argument kept asking: Why are we here, and couldn’t we have avoided this conflict? A rule that makes the government answer those questions is easy to administer. It is also faithful to the clause’s original meaning.

Disclosure: The author was amicus curiae in support of the petitioners in St. Mary Catholic Parish v. Roy.

Stephanie Barclay is a Professor of Law at Georgetown Law School, and the Faculty Director of the Georgetown Center for the Constitution.
